Title: On Local Inference for Functionals of Ill-Posed Inverse Problems

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19350v1

Published Time: Thu, 15 Oct 2026 00:38:06 GMT

Number of Pages: 196

Markdown Content:
# On Local Inference for Functionals of Ill-Posed Inverse Problems 

Ellen Graham ∗1 and Andrea Rotnitzky 21Department of Biostatistics, Epidemiology, and Informatics, University of Pennsylvania 

2Department of Biostatistics, University of Washington October 2026 

Abstract 

We study inference for continuous linear functionals of solutions to ill-posed inverse problems, includ-ing Euclidean parameters arising in nonparametric instrumental variables, proximal causal inference, and shadow-variable models. Although pointwise root-n inference is possible under standard conditions, we show that this need not translate into locally reliable inference. Under a model imposing only minimal regularity conditions, the target is Hellinger discontinuous when both inverse problems are ill-posed: arbitrarily small changes in the data-generating law can produce arbitrarily large changes in the tar-get, ruling out informative locally uniform inference. Imposing a uniform bound on a minimum-norm solution restores Hellinger continuity under regularity conditions, but does not by itself ensure stable root-n inference. If, in addition, both inverse problems are ill-posed and the solution whose norm is uniformly bounded is nonunique at a law P0, then under regularity conditions the target fails to be pathwise differentiable; consequently, asymptotically linear estimators exhibit nonvanishing root-n bias under some local alternatives, causing Wald confidence intervals to undercover asymptotically. Under regularity conditions, pathwise differentiability is restored if the uniformly bounded solution is unique at 

P0. It is also restored, regardless of uniqueness, under the stronger requirement that the corresponding solution satisfy a uniformly bounded β-source condition for some β > 0. We relate these regimes to very weak, weak, and strong identification in instrumental variables models. 

# 1 Introduction 

Many parameters of interest can be expressed as real-valued continuous linear functionals of solutions to a pair of integral operator equations, including estimands in nonparametric instrumental variables (NPIV) 

> ∗

Corresponding author: Ellen.Graham@PennMedicine.upenn.edu 

1arXiv:2610.19350v1 [math.ST] 14 Oct 2026 regression (Newey and Powell, 2003; Darolles et al., 2011; Blundell, Chen, and Kristensen, 2007; Horowitz, 2011; Severini and Tripathi, 2012; Bennett et al., 2023b; Bennett et al., 2023a; Olivas-Martinez and Rot-nitzky, 2025), in proximal causal inference (Cui et al., 2024), and in inference under nonignorable missingness using shadow variables (Miao and Tchetgen Tchetgen, 2018; Li, Miao, and Tchetgen, 2022). Such equations are often ill-posed in the sense of Hadamard (Hadamard, 1952): they may fail to admit a unique solution (Newey and Powell, 2003; Santos, 2012; Kallus, Mao, and Uehara, 2022), and even when a unique solution exists, it need not depend continuously on the inputs to the equation (Carrasco, Florens, and Renault, 2007). When the variables of interest are continuously distributed, this behavior is typical rather than exceptional (Carrasco, Florens, and Renault, 2007). Nevertheless, pointwise root-n asymptotically linear estimators of the target functional remain attainable through Neyman-orthogonal estimating equations whose bias is insensitive to first-order errors in the ill-posed solutions (Chernozhukov et al., 2018; Bennett et al., 2023b; Bennett et al., 2023a; Olivas-Martinez and Rotnitzky, 2025). A pointwise asymptotic guarantee at a fixed law P0 describes the behavior of an inferential procedure when the data are generated from exactly the same law P0 for every sample size as n → ∞ . However, at any given sample size n, there may be laws Pn whose Hellinger distance from P0 is sufficiently small that data generated under Pn cannot be reliably distinguished from data generated under P0 on the basis of n observations. This makes it important to ask whether the target parameter, denoted by φ(P ), is itself stable as P varies near P0. If arbitrarily small changes in the data-generating law, as measured by Hellinger distance, can produce nonvanishing, or even arbitrarily large, changes in φ(P ), then no inferential procedure can perform uniformly well over sufficiently small neighborhoods of P0. As established in a large body of work (Bahadur and Savage, 1956; Donoho, 1988; Donoho and Liu, 1991; Low, 1997; Cai and Low, 2004; Pfanzagl, 1998; P¨ otscher, 2002; Dufour, 1997), failure of Hellinger continuity has direct inferential consequences: point estimators must incur substantial error at some nearby laws, while confidence intervals that maintain coverage throughout such neighborhoods must remain correspondingly wide; in the most severe cases considered below, the expected diameter of any locally honest confidence interval is necessarily infinite. Even when P 7 → φ(P ) is Hellinger continuous at P0, continuity alone does not determine whether the parameter varies at a scale compatible with ordinary root-n inference. The standard way to study this question is to examine the behaviour of the target functional along each possible quadratic mean differentiable (QMD) submodel {Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} through P0 = Pt=0 (Vaart, 1998, Chapter 25). Along any such submodel with non-zero score, the Hellinger distance between Pt and P0 is of order t. Consequently, alternatives of the form Ph/ √n, for fixed h > 0, lie at the critical statistical scale: with n observations they remain distinguishable from P0, but not consistently distinguishable, in the sense that level-α Neyman–Pearson test of P0 against 2Ph/ √n has limiting power strictly between α and one. To study root-n inference under such alternatives, it is typical to assess the pathwise differentiability of the parameter φ. Pathwise differentiability requires that along each QMD submodel, t 7 → φ(Pt) is differentiable at t = 0, and that these derivatives depend on the submodel only through a bounded linear functional of the submodel’s score, called the local parameter, this bounded linear functional being common to all QMD submodels. For pathwise differentiable parameters, under some conditions (Klaassen, 1987), it is possible to construct asymptotically linear estimators whose normal limiting distributions are invariant to the local submodel, facilitating asymptotic inference based on this common limiting distribution that is asymptotically valid under sampling from any such local alternative. Conversely, when pathwise differentiability fails, for any asymptotically linear estimator, there are local alternatives under which the estimator has non-vanishing root-n bias, and the corresponding Wald intervals centered at asymptotically linear estimators undercover asymptotically under those local alternatives. For the target linear functionals that are the focus of the present paper, recent work has shown that Wald-type confidence intervals centered at estimators obtained from Neyman-orthogonal estimating equations are not globally uniformly valid over a minimal model that suffices for the existence of such estimating equations (Smucler, Robins, and Rotnitzky, 2025) when the dependence between the variables defining the operator equation is allowed to become arbitrarily weak and the range of the target parameter is unbounded. However, such global guarantees are strictly stronger than the local guarantees considered here. Global validity requires an inferential procedure to perform well uniformly over the entire model, including at laws that may be sufficiently far from P0 that, when P0 is the true law, they can be distinguished from P0 with probability tending to one as the sample size increases. Local validity, by contrast, only requires good behavior over laws that remain close to P0 and therefore cannot be so easily ruled out from the observed data. There are many settings in which locally valid inference is possible even though globally valid inference is necessarily degenerate. Examples include inference for the Wald estimand under arbitrarily weak instruments, for a ratio of means when the denominator is not uniformly bounded away from zero, and for the average treatment effect under arbitrarily poor overlap (Gleser and Hwang, 1987; Smucler, Lanni, and Bonet, 2026; Fieller, 1954; Khan and Tamer, 2010). By contrast, the question of locally valid inference for continuous linear functionals of ill-posed inverse problems has received comparatively little attention. In the present work, we investigate Hellinger continuity and pathwise differentiability of parameters defined as continuous linear functionals of solutions to ill-posed inverse problems under a baseline model that suffices for the existence of a Neyman-orthogonal estimating equation and three of its submodels that make stronger assumptions about the minimum norm solutions to the inverse problems. Below, we give an overview of our results, and briefly discuss their implications for inference. We begin by showing that, under the baseline model and some regularity conditions on P0, the target 3parameter is not Hellinger continuous over this model at points P0 where both inverse problems are ill-posed, which, as noted above, is typical when X and Z are continuous. The value of the parameter can diverge to infinity along a sequence of laws that converge in Hellinger distance to P0. Failure of continuity further implies failure of pathwise differentiability. Standard two-point arguments then imply that no locally uniformly consistent estimators exist, and that the expected diameter of all locally uniformly honest confidence intervals must be infinite at every sample size n (P¨ otscher, 2002). In summary, even though pointwise inference is possible within this baseline model, local inference is impossible. We additionally show that the maximal tangent space of this statistical model is equal to that of a locally non-parametric model under regularity conditions when the integral operators map between spaces of the same dimension. This result stands in contrast to related results that implicitly or explicitly compute tangent spaces only relative to the set of QMD at P0 submodels along which the minimum norm solution is differentiable (Severini and Tripathi, 2012; Chen and Santos, 2018). We then examine the target parameter in a smaller statistical model that additionally enforces that the minimum norm solution to one of the two inverse problems is bounded with a bound that holds uniformly over the model. This assumption is justified in several applications, such as the well-specified causal-NPIV model with bounded counterfactual outcomes and the shadow variable model under an overlap condition. We show that under a uniform norm bound, and under regularity conditions, Hellinger continuity of the target parameter is restored. We then investigate pathwise differentiability within a model where we assume the minimum norm solutions to one or both inverse problems are uniformly bounded. We show that even within this more restrictive model, the target parameter fails to be pathwise differentiable relative to the set of all one-dimensional QMD at P0 submodels, under regularity conditions, when there exist multiple solutions at P0

to whichever inverse problems are known to admit bounded solutions uniformly and both inverse problems are ill-posed. When only one minimum norm solution is bounded and the other inverse problem admits a unique solution at P0, our result requires ( X, Z ) to be continuous; for ( X, Z ) finite discrete, the parameter is then pathwise differentiable. Consequently, at laws where pathwise differentiability fails, as discussed above, all asymptotically linear estimators will exhibit non-vanishing root-n scaled bias under repeated sampling from some local submodel, and Wald confidence intervals centered at them will undercover asymptotically. In proving failure of pathwise differentiability in this model, we construct a submodel for which one singular value of the operators defining the inverse problems tends to 0 at a root-n rate. We discuss the relationship between this submodel and the analysis of weak instruments in the linear instrumental variables problem in Section 5. We further argue that this submodel gives intuition for the properties of estimators in the NPIV, proximal causal inference, and shadow variables models when instruments, proxies, and shadow 4variables, respectively, are only weakly informative relative to the sample size (see Section 5.8). In contrast, if the solution that admits a uniform norm bound is unique at P0, the parameter is pathwise differentiable relative to the set of all one-dimensional QMD at P0 submodels under regularity conditions. When no restrictions are placed on the solution to the other inverse problem, pathwise differentiability holds regardless of whether the other inverse problem admits a unique solution at P0. We also prove pathwise differentiability under the stronger model that assumes a uniform bound on the norm of the β-source representer of the primal (or symmetrically dual) solution for βh > 0 (respectively, βq > 0), a technical condition that is related to a strong-instrument assumption (see Section 5). Here, differentiability holds whether or not the primal (or symmetrically, dual) inverse problem admits a unique solution at P0. We further show that, in both cases, the maximal tangent space is no larger than the ones derived by Severini and Tripathi, 2012 and Chen and Santos, 2018 for related models. When there exist multiple solutions to the other inverse problem, the maximal tangent space is strictly smaller than that of a locally non-parametric model under mild regularity conditions, implying that some regular asymptotically linear estimators are locally inefficient at P0.To give intuition for the above results, we conduct a simple simulation study demonstrating the poor finite-sample performance of the Tikhonov-regularized DML estimator in a fully discrete NPIV problem when data are generated from local perturbations of a law P0 at which both inverse problems admit multiple solutions. We compare the performance under perturbations where the minimum norm solutions diverge to infinity, where they are uniformly bounded, and under repeated sampling from the fixed law P0.The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we fix notation, define the target functional together with the primal and dual inverse problems, review the relevant semiparametric theory, and review the implications of Hellinger discontinuity and of failure of pathwise differentiability for inference. In Section 3, we define the baseline statistical model, show that the target parameter is Hellinger discontinuous over this model when both inverse problems are ill-posed, apply the results of Section 2 to conclude that locally uniform inference is impossible, and compute the maximal tangent space. In Section 4, we consider submodels that uniformly bound the norms of the minimum norm solutions. We show that such bounds restore Hellinger continuity, give conditions under which pathwise differentiability fails and under which it holds, including under a uniform bound on the β-source representer, and bound the corresponding tangent spaces. In Section 5, we interpret these results by analogy with very weak, weak, and strong instrument settings in the linear instrumental variables model. In Section 6, we reconcile our results with existing pointwise results based on Neyman-orthogonal estimating equations, and distinguish Neyman orthogonality from pathwise differentiability. In Section 7, we illustrate failure of continuity and of pathwise differentiability in a finite discrete NPIV example with a small simulation study. We conclude in Section 8. All proofs are 5deferred to the supplement. 

# 2 Preliminaries 

2.1 Notation 

We first set notation used throughout. Let O = ( W, X, Z, D ) denote a Rℓ valued random variable defined over the Borel σ-algebra B(Rℓ), with W, X, Z, D taking values in RℓW , RℓX , RℓZ , NℓD , respectively, and ℓ := 

ℓW + ℓX + ℓZ + ℓD . Throughout, we assume that D is finite-discrete. Let P denote our statistical model, i.e. a collection of probability measures defined on the measurable space ( Rℓ, B(Rℓ)). We suppose that the true data generating process P0 is restricted only to belong to P, and that we observe n i.i.d. draws of O

distributed according to P0 ∈ P . Throughout we will assume all distributions P are supported on a bounded rectangle in Rℓ and absolutely continuous with respect to a product measure λ = λW ⊗ λX ⊗ λZ ⊗ λD . We let p := dP dλ be a version of the density of O with respect to λ, pX a version of the density of X with respect to λX , pX|Z a version of the conditional density of X given Z with respect to λX (e.g. pX,Z /p Z ), and pX|Z,d 

a version of the conditional density of X given Z, D = d with respect to λX . The parameter φ(P ) is well defined even when hP and qP are not uniquely defined, i.e. when there exist multiple solutions to the primal or dual inverse problem. Example 1 (Additive Nonparametric Instrumental Variables) . Suppose that W, X, Z are each one-dimensional random variables, where W is an outcome, X is a treatment, and Z is an instrumental variable. For simplicity, suppose that D is absent, though finite-discrete covariates could be incorporated through D.Consider the additive structural causal model 

W (x, z ) = hP ;cdr (x) + ϵW ;P , with EP (ϵW ;P | Z) = 0 , (6) where hP ;cdr is unspecified and W (x, z ) denotes the counterfactual outcome that would be observed if X and 

Z were set to x and z. This model imposes that Z has no direct effect on the counterfactual outcome and does not modify the effect of X. Hence W (x, z ) does not depend on z, and we write W (x) for this common counterfactual value. Since EP (ϵW ;P ) = 0, then 

hP ;cdr (x) = EP {W (x)}. (7) 9Thus, the function x 7 → hP ;cdr (x) is interpreted as the causal dose-response curve of treatment X. By consistency, the observed outcome satisfies W = W (X, Z ) = hP ;cdr (X) + ϵW ;P . Therefore, 

EP (W | Z) = EP {hP ;cdr (X) | Z}. (8) Thus the causal dose-response curve is a solution to the primal inverse problem 

EP h = rP with rP (Z) := EP (W | Z).

Given a known weight function ψ ∈ L2(PX ), the target parameter is the weighted mean of the causal dose-response curve, 

φ(P ) := EP {ψ(X)hP ;cdr (X)}.

2.3 Review of Semiparametric Theory 

Before giving our results, we give a brief review of semiparametric efficiency theory, including the definition of a tangent space, regular estimators, and pathwise differentiability. See Bickel et al., 1998 for a textbook treatment of this material. Suppose that distributions in a model P are dominated by the measure λ, as will be assumed throughout this work. Then, we say a one-dimensional parametric submodel {Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} ⊆ P passing through P0

with Pt=0 = P0 is quadratic mean differentiable (QMD) at P0 if there exists s ∈ L20(P0) with lim 

> t→0

∫ { √ pt(o) − √p0(o)

t − 12 s(o)√p0(o)

}2

dλ (o) = 0 .

The function s is the so called score, and generalizes the classical pointwise derivative of the log-likelihood function. We let P(P0, P) denote the set of all one dimensional QMD at P0 submodels of P. We define the maximal tangent set as the set of scores of submodels P(P0, P) and the maximal tangent space at P0 relative to P

as the closed linear span of the tangent set. We denote the maximal tangent set with ˙T (P0, P) and the maximal tangent space with T (P0, P). Note that the latter is a subspace of L20(P0). When the model consists of the set of all distributions for O, the maximal tangent space is equal to L20(P0). Consequently, whenever T (P0, P) = L20(P0), the model is said to be (locally) non-parametric at P0. When T (P0, P) is finite dimensional, the model is said to be (locally) parametric at P0. When T (P0, P) is neither equal to 

L20(P0) nor finite dimensional, the model is said to be (locally) semiparametric at P0.12 The set of submodels P(P0, P) can be used to state an efficiency theory for regular estimators of param-eters that are pathwise differentiable relative to P(P0, P). We first introduce pathwise differentiability for parameters that take values in R, then extend the definition to parameters that take values in a real separable Hilbert space. A real valued parameter P 7 → ϕ(P ) ∈ R is pathwise differentiable relative to P(P0, P) if there exists a bounded linear functional ˙ϕP0 : T (P0, P) → R such that, for all {Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} ∈ P(P0, P), ˙ϕP0 [s] = ddt ϕ(Pt)

We often drop ”relative to P(P0, P)” when discussing tangent spaces, pathwise differentiability, and regularity. In this work, these should all be taken relative to P(P0, P). Regular estimators achieve their limits in a locally uniform sense, where uniformity is with respect to the submodels in P(P0, P). The existence of regular estimators is intimately related to the pathwise differentiability of the parameter ϕ. If there exists a regular and asymptotically linear estimator of ϕ at P0,then ϕ is pathwise differentiable at P0 (Vaart, 1991). On the other hand, if ϕ is pathwise differentiable at P0,under some conditions and in particular if a consistent estimator of the gradient exists, there exist regular and asymptotically linear estimators of ϕ at P0 (Klaassen, 1987). When the model tangent space is L20(P0), all regular and asymptotically linear estimators share a single influence function, and hence are asymptotically equivalent; no regular estimator can exploit restrictions in P to gain asymptotic efficiency. When the tangent space is strictly included in L20(P0), some regular and asymptotically linear estimators will be more efficient than others, if they exist. The efficient estimators are exactly those that best exploit the modeling restrictions to estimate the parameter of interest. The above definitions apply to real-valued parameters. Many of the definitions extend readily to real separable Hilbert space-valued parameters. See Luedtke and Chung, 2024 for a detailed discussion of effi-ciency theory and estimation for such parameters. In particular, let H denote a fixed real separable Hilbert space, for example L2(P0). Suppose now that ϕ : P → H takes values in H. Then, we say that ϕ is pathwise differentiable relative to P(P0, P) if there exists a bounded linear operator ˙ϕP0 : T (P0, P) → H such that, for all QMD at P0 submodels {Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} ∈ P(P0, P), (10) holds where s is again the score of the submodel. As in the real valued setting, we call ˙ϕP0 the local parameter of ϕ at P0. In contrast to the real valued setting, when H is infinite dimensional, there does not exist an equivalent formulation in terms of gradients ϕ1

2.4 Parameter Smoothness and Implications for Inference 

A large body of classical asymptotic theory is devoted to the connection between the smoothness of a pa-rameter ϕ at P0 in a statistical model M, and the possibility or impossibility of inference about ϕ within the model M (Bahadur and Savage, 1956; Donoho, 1988; Donoho and Liu, 1991; Donoho, 1994; Gleser and Hwang, 1987; Low, 1997; Cai and Low, 2004; Pfanzagl, 1998; Dufour, 1997; P¨ otscher, 2002). Here, we briefly review the implications of two measures of smoothness: Hellinger continuity, and pathwise differen-tiability. Briefly, Hellinger discontinuity implies that no estimator is locally uniformly consistent, and that 14 no confidence interval can be locally uniformly honest while having diameter shrinking to zero. When the local modulus of continuity is infinite, as it is for our target parameter under the conditions of Section 3.1, every locally uniformly honest confidence interval must have infinite expected diameter at every sample size. Failure of pathwise differentiability implies that all asymptotically linear estimators are irregular, exhibiting non-vanishing local bias when data are generated from some local alternatives, leading to under-coverage of Wald-type confidence intervals under those alternatives. We leave a more in depth discussion to Supple-ment S.1. The forthcoming results should be taken as standard; our contribution is not their derivation, but their application to the parameter defined in Section 2.2. 

2.4.1 Implications of Hellinger Discontinuity 

To fix ideas, Hellinger continuity is defined as follows. 

Definition 2. We say that a real valued functional ϕ : M → R is Hellinger continuous at P ∈ M if, for all 

ϵ > 0, there exists δ > 0 such that for all P ′ ∈ M such that H(P ′, P ) < δ , it holds that |ϕ(P ) − ϕ(P ′)| < ϵ .We begin by giving general finite-sample consequences of Hellinger discontinuity. The following finite-sample bounds follow from elementary two-point testing arguments. They are in the same spirit as the lower bounds for confidence intervals developed by Low, 1997 and Cai and Low, 2004, and are closely related to the modulus-of-continuity approach of Donoho and Liu, 1991; Donoho, 1994. Given a collection M of probability laws defined on a common measurable space, the local modulus of continuity at P0 ∈ M of a parameter ϕ over M for a Hellinger radius r > 0 is defined as: 

ωP0 (r) = sup {| ϕ(P ) − ϕ(P0)| : H(P, P 0) < r, P ∈ M} .

The local modulus of continuity quantifies the local sensitivity, i.e. instability to small changes in the data generating distribution. A rapidly vanishing modulus (as r goes to 0) indicates local stability. On the other hand, a modulus that fails to vanish as r goes to 0 indicates discontinuity at P0. Indeed, it is immediate from the definition of continuity that at P0 such that ϕ is Hellinger discontinuous, there exists ϵ > 0 such that 

ωP0 (r) > ϵ for every r > 0 (see the definition of Hellinger continuity in Definition 2). This fact combined with a standard two-point argument leads to the conclusion that, at every fixed sample size, any confidence interval with honest coverage over a sufficiently small Hellinger neighborhood of P0 must have length bounded away from zero with positive probability under P0, and that the corresponding local uniform estimation error for point estimation is also bounded away from 0. Throughout, P n denotes the law of n i.i.d. draws from P ,

Cn denotes a random interval whose endpoints are measurable functions of n i.i.d. draws, and |Cn| denotes its diameter, allowing the value + ∞. We say that Cn has coverage at least 1 − α over a set A ⊆ M if 15 inf P ∈A P n{ϕ(P ) ∈ Cn} ≥ 1 − α. For the following result, define

It follows from the preceding lemma that, under Hellinger discontinuity, any finite-sample interval that is honest over a sufficiently small Hellinger neighborhood of a discontinuity point P0 must assign positive probability, under P0, to having length greater than ϵ, with ϵ independent of n. In particular, this immediately rules out Wald confidence intervals as having local coverage greater than or equal to their stated nominal level for sufficiently large n, as the diameter of Wald intervals converges to 0 as n → ∞ . We now study the implications of these finite-sample statements for locally uniform asymptotic inference at points P0 where ϕ is Hellinger discontinuous. Since τ can be taken arbitrarily small, the right-hand side can be made arbitrarily close to 1 − 2α.In summary, the failure of Hellinger continuity has three consequences. First, for every fixed sample size, honest confidence intervals over sufficiently small Hellinger neighborhoods must have length bounded away from zero with positive probability under P0. Second, point estimators have error bounded away from zero somewhere in those same neighborhoods. Third, as a result, locally uniform asymptotic estimation and locally uniform asymptotic confidence intervals with diameter that shrinks to zero are impossible, even over neighborhoods whose radii shrink to zero arbitrarily fast. 

2.4.2 Implications of Failure of Pathwise Differentiability 

Pathwise differentiability, formally defined in Section 2.3, is a strictly stronger smoothness condition than Hellinger continuity, see Remark S.1. While failure of pathwise differentiability has less severe consequences for inference than failure of Hellinger continuity, it nevertheless implies poor asymptotic coverage of Wald-type intervals for asymptotically linear estimators under sampling from some local alternative distributions. We explore these implications below. In what follows, let PΘ := {{ Pt,θ ∈ M : t ∈ [0 , δ θ )} : θ ∈ Θ} be a collection of QMD at P0 submodels of M where Θ is a (possibly infinite dimensional) index set. Let sθ be the score of the submodel {Pt,θ : t ∈

[0 , δ θ )} for each θ ∈ Θ. To analyze ϕ under sampling from local alternatives of the form P1/√n,θ , one decomposes the root-n error 17 as

If ̂ ϕn is root-n consistent at P0, the first term is OP0 (1) and, by contiguity, remains OP (1) under P1/√n,θ .Hence the root-n error can remain OP (1) only if the second term is O(1). If, moreover, ̂ ϕn is asymptoti-cally linear at P0 with influence function ϕ1

# 3 The Baseline Statistical Model 

We now define P, the largest statistical model we consider over which we desire to perform statistical inference, of which all subsequent models are submodels. Our choice of P suffices for the existence of a Neyman orthogonal estimating equation of the form (4). However, this model does not encode any additional restrictions that may be implied by certain structural causal models, as in the case of NPIV and the shadow variable model. We return to this point in Section 4.1 below. As we will show in Section 3.1, the mapping P 7 → φ(P ) is Hellinger discontinuous over P at distributions 

P0 satisfying mild conditions. Combined with Section 2.4.1, such discontinuity implies that local uniform inference is impossible over P at such P0. In Section 3.2 we derive the tangent space within this model, and discuss the relationship between the present results and the related works Severini and Tripathi, 2012; Chen and Santos, 2018. Our first assumption concerns the smoothness of the functionals in (2). 

Assumption 1. At P , the functionals (2) are bounded and linear. By the Riesz representation theorem, Assumption 1 suffices for the existence of the Riesz representers 19 αP and rP . This assumption is satisfied under mild conditions on P for each of Example 1, Example 3, and Example 2, as can be seen from application of Example 5 in Luedtke and Chung, 2024. The next assumption concerns the subspaces to which these Riesz representers belong. 

Assumption 2. At P , rP ∈ R(EP ) and αP ∈ R(E∗ 

> P

), equivalently there exist solutions to (3). Bennett et al., 2023a show that Assumption 2 suffices for identification of φ(P ) even when there exist multiple solutions to (3), and for the validity of the doubly robust expansion of the estimating equation (4) from which DML estimators are constructed. Assumptions 1 and 2 are the minimal assumptions required to define the parameters considered in Section 2.2 via the dual formulation we give. Nevertheless, they should not be taken as a given. When X, Z are continuously valued, Assumption 2 places intricate assumptions on the structure of rP , αP , and the joint distribution of ( X, Z, D ): Informally, rP and αP must not be too strongly supported on the tailing singular vectors of the singular value decomposition of the operator EP for each P .For inference based on (4), we require that hP and qP can be estimated sufficiently fast as the sample size grows. A typical regularity condition to facilitate fast estimation rates is the so-called β-source conditions (Carrasco, Florens, and Renault, 2007; Bennett et al., 2023b; Olivas-Martinez and Rotnitzky, 2025): 

Assumption 3. Let βh, β q ∈ [0 , ∞). At P ,

βh = 0, βq = 1 as Strong Identification. Higher values of βh, βq imply stronger smoothness of the solutions, as they ensure that hP and qP concentrate less mass on the tailing singular vectors of EP . Higher values of

Bennett et al., 2023b and Olivas-Martinez and Rotnitzky, 2025 analyze the behavior of DML estimators based on (4) under various values of βh, βq .We also require that every distribution in P be mutually absolutely continuous relative to a reference distribution P0, whose role is made precise following (20) below. 20 Assumption 4. For example, without uniform upper and lower bounds on the density of O under P , there may exist functions g ∈ L2(P0) such that even the mean functional P 7 → EP [g(O)] fails to be Hellinger continuous (Bahadur and Savage, 1956). Assumption 5 allows for discrete shared covariates D between the primal and dual inverse problems in (3). However, because the joint distribution of ( X, Z ) under each P is absolutely continuous with respect to a product measure, supported on a rectangle, and with density bounded below by a constant, shared continuous covariates are not allowed. In other words, we do not allow X = ( X′, R ), Z = ( Z′, R ) with R arandom variable. Because D is only able to accommodate discrete covariates, exclusion of such R prevents inclusion of continuous covariates in Example 1, Example 2, and Example 3. We leave an extension of the forthcoming results to settings with shared continuous covariates to future work. We are now able to define the statistical model we consider inference over. 

P := {P a distribution for O such that Assumptions 1, 2, 3, 4, and 5 hold } (20) In the above definition, Assumption 3 is assumed to hold for a fixed but arbitrary choice of βh, β q ∈ [0 , ∞), and Assumption 4 relative to the reference distribution P0. As the forthcoming results hold for any choice of βh, β q ∈ [0 , ∞) and P0 ∈ P , we suppress this dependence in the notation. 21 Of these assumptions, Assumption 1, Assumption 2, and Assumption 3 are standard in the literature on inference for these parameters, while Assumption 4 and Assumption 5 are regularity conditions of our own. In restricting the laws available to perturb toward P0, they only strengthen the impossibility results that follow: Hellinger discontinuity of φ at P0 over P implies discontinuity at P0 over any model containing P,and the maximal tangent space at P0 can only be larger over a larger model. In the remainder of this section, we give the main results of the paper. Under the model P, the parameter 

φ(P ) is not Hellinger continuous at P0 satisfying mild regularity conditions. We will further argue that the maximal tangent space of the model P at P0 is equal to L20(P0) under mild regularity conditions, implying these assumptions impose no first order restriction on the model. We then consider continuity and pathwise differentiability under various restrictions of the model P.

3.1 Failure of Continuity over the Baseline Model 

We now demonstrate that the parameter φ(P ) is not Hellinger continuous over P defined in (20) at P0

satisfying mild regularity conditions. Recall the definition of Hellinger continuity in Definition 2. In fact, we will prove a stronger result than lack of continuity at P : that there exists a sequence {Pj } ⊆ P such that lim j→∞ H2(Pj , P ) = 0 but where lim j→∞ |φ(Pj ) − φ(P )| = ∞.We split our results for this subsection into two pieces. First, we consider the case where X, Z are finite-discretely valued, then consider the case where X, Z are continuously valued. For X, Z discrete, we have the following result.

}†αPj converge to the minimum norm solutions at P0, and so lim j→∞ φ(Pj ) = 

φ(P0). Convergence of the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverses holds if and only if the operators EP , E∗ 

> P

have constant rank locally at P0 over the model P. In finite dimensions, full rank is preserved under small perturbations. However, when EP , E∗ 

> P

are not full rank, i.e. they both have non-trivial null spaces, an arbitrarily small perturbation to P0 can change the rank of the operators. Under such perturbations, the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse is discontinuous at P0, and the minimum norm solutions to the inverse problems (3) can diverge to infinity, which can in turn cause φ(P ) to diverge. The sequence {Pj }∞ 

> j=1

we construct along which 

|φ(Pj )| diverges to infinity is one such sequence where the rank of EPj ;de is greater than the rank of EP0;de

for every j. Informally, the rank of the operator EPj ;de encodes how much information X and Z carry about each other. When the rank decreases, X and Z provide an order of magnitude less information about each other. Hence, discontinuity of the parameter occurs when information relating X and Z can be lost at P0

relative to laws near P0. In Section 5, we relate this phenomenon to the very weak instrument setting in linear instrumental variables models. part (a) of the above lemma further requires that rP0 (·, d e) and αP0 (·, d e) are not constant functions for some de for which both inverse problems are ill-posed. Without this assumption, the parameter at P0

degenerates to the P0-mean of a known function. We note that when W, X, Z are discrete, the laws P in P at which EP ;d fails to have full rank for some 

d form a set of measure zero. To make this precise, identify EP ;d with the KZ × KX row-stochastic matrix whose ( z, x ) entry is P (X = x|Z = z, D = d), so that each row lies in the simplex of probability vectors on 

{1, . . . , K X }. With respect to the uniform measure on the resulting product of simplices, the rank deficient matrices form a set of measure zero, since failure of full rank requires the vanishing of a polynomial in the entries that does not vanish identically. On the other hand, when X and Z are continuous, failure of continuity is the typical case, as demonstrated in Theorem 1 below. Before giving the Theorem statement, we introduce a further regularity condition for the functions m, ˜m to satisfy at P0.23 Assumption 6. Suppose that ˜m, m, P 0 satisfy that there exists 0 < L < ∞ such that for all h ∈ L1(PX,D ;0 ), 

q ∈ L1(PZ,D ;0 ), 

∥ ˜m(W, X, D ; h)∥L1(PW,X,D ;0 ) ≤ L∥h∥L1(PX,D ;0 ), ∥m(W, Z, D ; q)∥L1(PW,Z,D ;0 ) ≤ L∥q∥L1(PZ,D ;0 ).

Assumption 6 is a technical condition used in the proofs of the results below. In particular, for any 

P mutually absolutely continuous with P0 and with density bounded above and below by a constant, it implies that P satisfies Assumption 1 and that the Riesz representers αP and rP are essentially bounded (Lemma S.6). It further implies that if such a sequence {Pj }∞ 

> j=1

converges to P0 in Hellinger distance, with densities bounded above and below by a constant not depending on j, then αPj and rPj converge weakly to 

αP0 and rP0 (Lemma S.7). Under mild conditions, this condition is satisfied for NPIV, for proximal causal inference, and for the shadow variable model.

We are now ready to state our discontinuity theorem in the continuous case. 

Theorem 1. Suppose P is as in (20) and P0 ∈ P . Suppose m, ˜m, P0 satisfy Assumption 6. Suppose the joint distribution of (X, Z )|D = d under P0 is dominated by the Lebesgue product measure for each d ∈

Supp (D; P0). Suppose rP0 (·, d ) and αP0 (·, d ) are not constant functions P0-a.e for some d ∈ Supp (D; P0).Then, φ is not Hellinger continuous at P0 over the model P, and there exists a sequence of distributions 

{Pj }∞ 

> j=1

⊆ P such that lim j→∞ H2(Pj , P 0) = 0 but where lim j→∞ |φ(Pj ) − φ(P0)| = ∞.

Under Assumption 5, when ( X, Z )|D = d are continuous random variables for each d, the operators EP0

and E∗ 

> P0

are both compact. As such, the inverse problems (3) are ill-posed: when EP0 has infinite rank, holding the operators fixed, arbitrarily small perturbations of the right hand sides rP0 , α P0 can produce arbitrarily large changes in the solutions hP0 and qP0 .Failure of continuity of φ arises instead from discontinuity of the operator valued mappings P 7 → E † 

> P

,

P 7 → {E ∗ 

> P

}†. Even though the operator valued mappings P 7 → E P , P 7 → E ∗ 

> P

are continuous at P0, when the inverse problems are ill-posed, the mapping taking each law to the corresponding pseudo-inverses E† 

> P

, {E ∗ 

> P

}†

may be discontinuous at P0. Such discontinuity can lead to discontinuity of the parameter even when the Riesz representers rP , α P do not depend on P , as with αP = ψ in Example 1 and rP (Z, D ) = DZ in Example 3. The discrete and continuous settings differ in how typical the discontinuity is. When X, Z are finite discrete, the maps P 7 → E † 

> P

, P 7 → {E ∗ 

> P

}† are discontinuous at P0 only when the rank of EP0 can change under an arbitrarily small perturbation of P0, and so arises only at the measure zero set of laws described above. When X, Z are continuous and the joint density is bounded above and below by a constant, the maps P 7 → E † 

> P

, P 7 → {E ∗ 

> P

}† are guaranteed to be discontinuous at P0. No restriction imposed by the model P prevents this discontinuity, including the β-source conditions. In our construction of the sequence 25 {Pj }∞ 

> j=1

⊆ P along which φ(Pj ) → ∞ , the operators EPj , E∗ 

> Pj

are finite rank for each j, and hence satisfy Assumption 3 for every choice of βh, β q ∈ [0 , ∞). As in the discrete case, we require that αP0 (·, d ) and 

rP0 (·, d ) are not constant functions so that φ(P0) does not degenerate to the mean of a fixed function at P0.The proof of Theorem 1 admits an interpretation in terms of weak instruments, which we develop in Section 5. Informally, the singular functions of EP act as canonical treatment and instrument contrasts, and compactness of EP produces infinitely many such contrasts that are arbitrarily weakly instrumented. The approximating laws in the proof place signal on a contrast whose strength tends to zero faster than the target-relevant mass placed on it, so that a Hellinger-small perturbation of the observed law, though hard to detect statistically, produces an unbounded change in the estimand. In this sense, Theorem 1 can be read as a continuum analogue of a many-weak-instruments phenomenon. Lemma 3 and Theorem 1 have severely negative implications for inference, as they imply that under the conditions of those results, the local modulus of continuity ωP0 (r) of φ over P is infinite for every r > 0. By Lemma 1, at such points P0, locally uniform inference over P is impossible for each n: the expected diameter of every locally uniformly honest confidence interval is infinite, and for every M > 0, every estimator has error exceeding M with probability at least (1 − τ )/2 at some law in a Hellinger neighborhood of P0 of radius 

rn,τ . Asymptotically, no estimator is locally uniformly consistent, and every confidence interval with locally uniform asymptotic coverage has diameter that is not bounded in probability under P0.

3.2 The Model Tangent Spaces 

We conclude this section by deriving the maximal tangent space of the model P at selected laws P0. As we will see, the tangent space is unrestricted when L2(PX,D ;0 ) and L2(PZ,D ;0 ) have the same dimension, meaning that the model P is unrestricted in a local sense at such P0. Note that when X and Z are continuous, dim( L2(PX,D ;0 )) = dim( L2(PZ,D ;0 )) automatically. This gives some further intuition on why the assumptions on the model P do not suffice for locally uniformly valid inference. The present results stand in contrast to related work that implicitly or explicitly derives the tangent space relative only to particularly smooth submodels of P (Severini and Tripathi, 2012; Chen and Santos, 2018). We still consider it instructive to derive this tangent space even though P 7 → φ(P ) is Hellinger discontinuous over this model, and hence not pathwise differentiable (Remark S.1), and so the tangent space cannot be used to gain insights into the behavior of regular estimators of φ(P ). As in the discussion of continuity, we begin with the discrete case. We only give our first result for 

KX = KZ .

Lemma 4. Suppose P is as in (20) and P0 ∈ P . Suppose that X is supported on {1, . . . , K X } and Z is 

26 supported on {1, . . . , K Z } with KX = KZ . Then, T (P0, P) = L20(P0).

The above lemma reveals the model P is locally non-parametric at any P0 in the model. In particular, Assumption 2, the assumption that there exist solutions to the primal and dual inverse problems, does not locally restrict the model, even when there exist multiple solutions to both the primal and dual inverse problems at P0. Intuitively, this result follows from identifying EP and E∗ 

> P

with KX ×KX matrices. Solutions to the primal and dual inverse problems exist whenever αP , r P , represented as vectors, are in the range of these matrices. Such solutions trivially exist if these matrices are invertible. But, every KX × KX matrix is arbitrarily close in Frobenius norm to an invertible KX × KX matrix. Upon establishing a relation between Frobenius norm and Hellinger distance, it becomes clear that every law at which solutions do not exist is arbitrarily close in Hellinger distance to a law where solutions do exist. One can show, as we do in the proof of Lemma 4, that such result implies a locally non-parametric tangent space at every P0 ∈ P .The above intuition persists in the case where X, Z are both continuous. In such cases, the spaces 

L2(PZ,D ) and L2(PX,D ) are the same dimension. Again, every law P at which there do not exist solutions to the primal and/or dual inverse problems is arbitrarily close in Hellinger distance to a law P ′ for which solutions exist. The technical argument is much more involved in this case, because Assumption 5 precludes 

EP and E∗ 

> P

from admitting a bounded inverse. Nevertheless, an appropriate adaptation can be made, from which we arrive at the following theorem. 

Theorem 2. Suppose P is as in (20) and P0 ∈ P . Suppose m, ˜m, P 0 satisfy Assumption 6. Suppose the joint distribution of (X, Z )|D = d under P0 is dominated by the Lebesgue product measure for each d ∈ Supp [PD;0 ].Then, T (P0, P) = L20(P0).

Lemma 4 and Theorem 2 diverge from existing results computing tangent spaces within models similar to P, for example Severini and Tripathi, 2012 and Chen and Santos, 2018. Such divergence arises because those authors only derived tangent spaces relative to the set of submodels along which the derivatives of particular solutions hP to the primal inverse problem exist. For concreteness, consider the following set of submodels: 

Ph† 

> Pdiff

(P0, P) := {{ Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} : {Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} ∈ P(P0, P), ddt h†

|t=0 exists } (21) where P(P0, P) is the set of all one-dimensional QMD at P0 submodels of P and the derivative is taken in L2(PX,D ;0 ). The proofs of Severini and Tripathi, 2012 only consider scores of submodels in a related set of submodels, but with different assumptions on P. Chen and Santos, 2018 also consider differentiability relative to a related set of submodels, see Condition A in that work. 27 Restricting to such submodels, those authors show the tangent space is typically restricted when

# 4 Models with Uniform Norm Bounds 

We now consider properties of φ relative to submodels of the model P that uniformly bound the minimum norm solutions to either the primal, the dual, or both the primal and dual inverse problems. 

4.1 Recovery of Continuity under Uniform Norm Bounds 

As shown in Lemma 3, a sufficient condition for Hellinger continuity of P 7 → φ(P ) is that at P0, EP0;d is full rank for each d. More generally, for ( X, Z ) discrete or continuous, uniformly bounding the norm of the minimum norm solutions of the inverse problems (3), together with further regularity conditions, suffices to recover continuity. We first give our assumption bounding the norm of the minimum norm solutions to the inverse problems (3). We consider two versions, one in which h† 

> P

is uniformly bounded and one in which q† 

> P

is uniformly 30 bounded.

Pcont .

Theorem 3 implies that the impossibility results of Section 3.1 do not apply over models on which the minimum-norm solution to at least one of the two inverse problems is uniformly bounded at points P0 where the Riesz representer maps are continuous. Assumption 7 should therefore be read as a substantive stability condition on at least one of the bridge functions that appear in the definition of φ(P ). It says that, uniformly over the model, the primal bridge (part (a)) or dual bridge (part (b)) can be chosen to have moderate L2

size. Equivalently, the integral equation is not allowed to be solved only by bridge functions with very large positive and negative values that cancel after conditioning on the proxy variables. In the language of Section 5, the bound on the primal bridge plays the role of a uniform bound on the structural coefficient in a linear instrumental variables model. We next discuss the plausibility of Assumption 7 in Example 1, then in a further example involving a misspecified NPIV model, and finally in Example 2 and Example 3. 

Example 1 (continued) . As pointed out in Example 1, under the additive structural NPIV model (6), the causal dose-response function hP,cdr solves the primal inverse problem, i.e. it satisfies (8). Thus, because equality (7) holds, when the absolute value of the counterfactual outcome W (x) is uniformly bounded over x

and over the model by a constant B, we have that, under any P that obeys the model, ∥hP ;cdr ∥L∞(PX ) < B .Since h† 

> P

is the minimum-norm solution, then ∥h† 

> P

∥L2(PX ) ≤ ∥ hP ;cdr ∥L2(PX ). Thus Assumption 7 part (a) follows from a transparent substantive condition: the causal dose-response curve is uniformly bounded. In this setting, Assumption 7 is not merely a technical restriction; it is implied by the structural interpretation of the bridge under the additive NPIV model and boundedness of the counterfactual outcomes. 

Example 4 (Misspecified NPIV model) . Suppose now that the structural model is allowed to include a direct effect of the instrument: 

W (x, z ) = hP ;cdr (x) + uP (z) + ϵW ;P , EP (ϵW ;P | Z) = 0 ,

where hP ;cdr is unrestricted and uP is normalized so that EP {uP (Z)} = 0. The term uP (z) represents a violation of the exclusion restriction. Indeed, for any fixed x, the contrast W (x, z )−W (x, z ′) = uP (z)−uP (z′)is the individual direct effect of changing the instrument from z′ to z while holding the treatment fixed at x.Because uP depends on the counterfactual outcomes and is not identified from the observed data without the exclusion restriction, throughout this example we let P index the law of the full data, including the counterfactual outcomes W (x, z ). 32 Let W (x, ·) := W (x, Z ) denote the counterfactual outcome when X is set to x but Z is left at its natural value. Since EP {uP (Z)} = 0 and EP (ϵW ;P ) = 0, the structural model implies that EP {W (x, ·)} = hP ;cdr (x). Thus x 7 → hP ;cdr (x) retains the interpretation of a causal dose-response curve, now averaging over the natural distribution of Z. In particular, if the counterfactual outcome is uniformly bounded over the model, then 

hP ;cdr is uniformly bounded as well. Suppose further that the direct effect of the instrument is mild, in the sense that |uP (z) − uP (z′)| ≤ δ

for all z, z ′. Since uP (Z) has mean zero and takes values in an interval of length at most δ, it follows that 

∥uP ∥L2(PZ ) ≤ δ/ 2. Thus this condition gives a clear sense in which the violation of the exclusion restriction is small on the Z-scale. The observed regression of W on Z satisfies EP (W | Z) = EP {hP ;cdr (X) | Z} + uP (Z). Equivalently, 

hP ;cdr solves the correctly specified inverse problem with right-hand side EP (W | Z) − uP (Z). Now suppose that the parameter of interest is the weighted average 

φ(P ) = EP {ψ(X)hP ;cdr (X)},

where ψ belongs to the range of E∗ 

> P

. Suppose also that uP = EP sP for some sP ∈ L2(PX ). Then hP ;cdr + sP

solves the misspecified inverse problem ( EP h)( Z) = EP (W | Z), which therefore admits a solution. If, incorrectly, we impose the exclusion restriction uP = 0, then we instead target 

φbias (P ) := EP {ψ(X)hP ;bias (X)},

where hP ;bias is any solution of the misspecified inverse problem ( EP h)( Z) = EP (W | Z). The range condition on ψ ensures that this functional is well-defined, in the sense that it does not depend on which solution of the misspecified inverse problem is chosen. A crucial point is that the solutions to the misspecified inverse problem may fail to be uniformly bounded in norm, and thus may fail to satisfy Assumption 7. To see this, recall that boundedness of the counterfactual outcome implies that hP ;cdr is uniformly bounded over the model, and the mild-direct effect condition implies that uP is uniformly small in L2(PZ ). However, neither fact implies that the solutions of ( EP h)( Z) = EP (W |

Z) have uniformly bounded L2(PX ) norm. Specifically, recall that hP ;cdr +sP solves the misspecified equation, where uP = EP sP . Because EP is ill-posed, sP may have very large L2(PX ) norm even when uP has very small L2(PZ ) norm. Consequently, the minimal norm solution of the misspecified inverse problem need not satisfy Assumption 7 part (a), even when the causal dose-response curve hP ;cdr is uniformly bounded. For a fixed law P , the difference between the misspecified and correctly specified targets admits an easy 33 to establish bound. Indeed, if qP is the minimum norm solution to E∗ 

> P

qP = ψ, then 

φbias (P ) − φ(P ) = EP {qP (Z)uP (Z)}.

Therefore, by Cauchy–Schwarz, 

|φbias (P ) − φ(P )| ≤ ∥ qP ∥L2(PZ )∥uP ∥L2(PZ ). (26) Consequently, at a fixed law P , as the direct effect uP tends to zero in L2(PZ ), then the bias |φbias (P )−φ(P )|

also goes to zero. That is, at each fixed law P , small violations of the exclusion restriction produce small representation bias. This point was raised by Deaner, 2022. Theorem 1.4 of that paper is a consequence of the inequality (26). This pointwise sensitivity statement is not, however, a local Hellinger continuity statement. To obtain a bound that is uniform over laws in a Hellinger neighborhood of P following the argument of Deaner, 2022, one would need a constant C < ∞ such that ∥qP ′ ∥L2(P ′  

> Z)

≤ C for all nearby laws P ′. This is a local version of Assumption 7 part (b). However, this is an assumption on the solution of the dual inverse-problem with no apparent substantive interpretation in this setting. In fact, without Assumption 7, the discontinuity results of Section 3.1 may apply and one may construct laws Pj converging to P in Hellinger distance for which the misspecified parameter φbias (Pj ) diverges to ∞. On the other hand, if the correctly specified target φ(P ) is Hellinger continuous, then φ(Pj ) → φ(P ). Along such a sequence it must be that 

|φbias (Pj ) − φ(Pj )| → ∞ .

This does not contradict the inequality (26). Rather, it shows that ∥qPj ∥L2(PZ;j ) must be diverging. 

Example 2 (continued) . Assumption 7 is difficult to assess in the proximal causal inference application of Example 2. There, the bridge functions are generally not themselves causal dose-response curves and we are not aware of any substantive interpretation for them. Even when the observed outcome is bounded, an outcome bridge need not be uniformly bounded: a conditional expectation equation can sometimes be solved only by a function with large positive and negative values that cancel after averaging over the proxy distribution. On the other hand, under a correctly specified proximal causal model, if the outcome W

is bounded, the ATE φ(P ) = EP [W (d = 1) − W (d = 0)] is bounded. Consequently, our constructions in Lemma 3 part (a) and Theorem 1 along which φ(Pj ) → ∞ must violate the proximal causal model. The specifics of this violation are important to investigate. If one or both bridge functions are truly uniformly 34 bounded under the causal model, the parameter φ will be Hellinger continuous. Alternatively, it could be possible that the causal model only restricts φ to belong to [min(Ω W ) − max(Ω W ), max(Ω W ) − min(Ω W )], where Ω W denotes the support of W , without sufficiently restricting the model to give Hellinger continuity of φ. In any case, as in the setting of misspecified NPIV, it seems plausible that even mild deviations from the proximal causal model could return us to the setting of Section 3.1. 

Example 3 (continued) . As noted in Example 3, the likelihood ratio qP (Z) := {P (D = 0) /P (D =1) }{ dQ Z /dU }(Z) solves the dual integral equation (9). Thus, in this example, Assumption 7 part (b) on the dual bridge is implied by a positivity-like overlap condition between the respondent and nonrespon-dent outcome distributions, namely a uniform bound on ∥qP ∥L2(U ). This overlap condition rules out, in an 

L2(U ) sense, the possibility that values of Z that occur only rarely among respondents can occur with high probability among nonrespondents. 

4.2 Failure of Pathwise Differentiability under Uniform Norm Bounds 

Hellinger continuity of φ was recovered in Section 4.1 by bounding uniformly over the model the minimum norm solution of the primal inverse problem, or symmetrically by bounding uniformly the minimum norm solution of the dual inverse problem. We now show that, under regularity conditions, these uniform norm bounds alone do not suffice to ensure pathwise differentiability. Under the model Ph† 

> Pbound

, pathwise differentiability fails at laws P0 where both inverse problems are ill-posed if the primal inverse problem admits multiple solutions, as we show below. Even under the stronger model Ph†  

> P,q †
> Pbound

On the other hand, in Section 4.3 below, we consider conditions under which pathwise differentiability holds when both inverse problems are ill-posed. We show that at P0 where the primal inverse problem admits a unique solution, φ is pathwise differentiable at P0 relative to Ph† 

> Pbound

, under regularity conditions. If we instead assume the stronger model that uniformly bounds the norm of the β-source representer of the primal solution, we show that pathwise differentiability holds whether or not the primal solution is unique. In both cases, we provide a bound on the tangent spaces. Analogous results can be stated in terms of the dual inverse problem. In the statistical literature, assumptions that the solutions to the primal and/or dual inverse problems are unique are often referred to as completeness assumptions Newey and Powell, 2003; Darolles et al., 2011; Horowitz, 2011. When ( X, Z ) are finite discrete, such completeness assumptions hold under mild assumptions for X, Z taking the same number of levels. However, for ( X, Z ) continuous, such completeness assumptions are difficult to reason about. See Hu and Shiu, 2018 for some sufficient conditions for completeness. Difficulty arises in part because, within the class of distributions considered by Canay, Santos, and Shaikh, 2013, at any distribution P for which the completeness assumptions hold, there exists another distribution P ′, arbitrarily close to P in Hellinger distance, for which the completeness assumptions do not hold. Consequently, in light of Le Cam’s two point method, for continuous ( X, Z ) it is impossible to construct a valid test of the null hypothesis that completeness fails with power exceeding its size against any alternative at which completeness holds (Canay, Santos, and Shaikh, 2013). Such difficulty has been extensively discussed in the setting of NPIVs, where the existence of a unique solution to the primal inverse problem is equivalent to identification of the structural function of interest, instead of simply certain linear functionals of it (Santos, 2012; Severini and Tripathi, 2012). Our present results show that even if uniqueness of solutions is not required for √n-asymptotically linear inference, uniqueness of the solution whose norm is uniformly bounded is required for regular asymptotically linear inference under the uniform norm bounds of Assumption 7 alone. As a consequence, Wald-type confidence intervals based on the standard DML estimator under-cover asymptotically under some local alternatives (Lemma 2), a fact which is hidden by a pointwise asymptotic analysis of the estimator. We show this poor behavior in a simple simulation in Section 7 below. In the following results, we assume the Riesz representers P 7 → αP , P 7 → rP are pathwise differentiable at

We note that this assumption is trivially true when X, Z are finite discrete and those null spaces are non-trivial. Theorem 4 and Theorem 5 have implications for inference. Under the conditions of those theorems, all asymptotically linear estimators, and in particular the estimator (5), are irregular: their limiting distributions are sensitive to vanishing perturbations of the true data generating process. In particular, by Lemma 2, under the conditions of either theorem, every asymptotically linear estimator exhibits non-vanishing local asymptotic bias along some sequence of local alternatives, so that Wald-type confidence intervals under-cover asymptotically. See also the references on irregular estimation in Section 2.4.2. We illustrate Lemma 2 using the proof of Theorem 5, which exhibits a collection of QMD at P0 submodels 

{{ Pt,γ : t ∈ [0 , δ )} : γ ∈ (a1, a 2)} with scores sγ = s∗ + γ¯s affine in γ (where s∗, ¯s ∈ L2(P0) \ { 0}), but with derivatives ddt φ(Pt,γ )∣∣t=0 = μγ for some μ̸ = 0. For any asymptotically linear estimator ̂φ n with influence function φ1 

> P0

> P0

> P0

Because μ̸ = 0, γ∆γ is a non-zero polynomial of degree at most two in γ, so ∆ γ = 0 for at most two γ ∈

(a1, a 2). Hence every asymptotically linear estimator is locally asymptotically biased along all but at most two of these submodels, and the corresponding Wald-type confidence intervals under-cover asymptotically along them. We demonstrate poor coverage in a small simulation in Section 7 under a related data generating process, where the √n-scaled bias is essentially unchanged between n = 200 and n = 200 ,000 while the 38 coverage of a nominal 95% Wald interval falls from 65% to 55% over those sample sizes. In Section 5, we relate this phenomenon to weak-instrument asymptotics in linear instrumental variables models.

We note the similarity between the present result and the results in Severini and Tripathi, 2012 and Chen and Santos, 2018. As discussed in Section 3.2, those authors computed a tangent space only relative to the submodels in a set similar to (21). Our results do not restrict attention to the set of submodels in (21). > P0

While it may be possible to test for violations of Assumption 8, a similar phenomenon to the impossibility of testing completeness (Canay, Santos, and Shaikh, 2013) likely applies for testing the converse when X, Z 

are continuous. Informally, it should be possible to perturb the tail of the singular value decomposition of 

EP to violate Assumption 8 while changing the law P only negligibly in Hellinger distance, at least under certain regularity conditions. The assumption is easiest to assess in the finite discrete case. as measuring the strength of these canonical contrasts. Then, Assumption 8 requires that max

# 5 Relation to Weak Instruments 

A useful way to interpret the preceding results is through their connection with weak-instrument phenomena in linear instrumental variables models. We begin by recalling the familiar one-treatment, one-instrument 41 linear IV problem, and then show that the singular-value decomposition of the NPIV conditional expectation operator transforms the NPIV problem into an infinite collection of canonical linear IV problems. This representation also clarifies an important distinction between the present setting and ordinary scalar IV: the structural function may contain unidentified components, while the target functional remains identified because it places zero weight on those components. 

5.1 The Scalar Linear IV Model 

Consider first the homoskedastic linear IV model with one endogenous treatment X and one instrument Z,

W = Xθ P + ϵP , X = Zσ P + ξP , EP (ϵP | Z) = EP (ξP | Z) = 0 , (30) where θP is the structural coefficient and σP is the first-stage coefficient. We allow EP (ϵP | X)̸ = 0, so that ordinary least squares need not identify θP .Substituting the first-stage equation into the structural equation gives a reduced-form regression of W

on Z with coefficient δP = σP θP . Thus, whenever σP̸ = 0, 

θP = δP

σP

. (31) Population two-stage least squares recovers precisely this ratio, and sample two-stage least squares replaces 

δP and σP by their estimated reduced-form and first-stage coefficients. The weak-instrument problem is therefore fundamentally a ratio problem. When σP is bounded away from zero, the map ( δP , σ P ) 7 → δP /σ P is smooth. When σP is close to zero, small changes in the first-stage and reduced-form coefficients can produce large changes in their ratio. Different asymptotic regimes formalize different forms of this instability. In the very-weak-instrument setting, the model permits σP to be arbitrarily close to, or equal to, zero without a restriction preventing 

δP /σ P from becoming arbitrarily large. Gleser and Hwang, 1987 and Dufour, 1997 show that this has severe consequences for uniformly valid inference. In the weak-instrument asymptotics of Staiger and Stock, 1997, the data-generating law is allowed to depend on n and the first-stage coefficient is local to zero, for example σP (n) = η/ √n. The reduced-form coefficient is then also of order 1 /√n when the structural coefficient remains of order one. Standard Wald inference based on two-stage least squares is not uniformly approximated by the usual strong-IV limit theory in this regime. In the classical strong-instrument regime, the relevant first-stage coefficient remains locally separated from zero and the ordinary smooth asymptotic theory applies. 42 We now show that these phenomena have direct analogues in the inverse problems studied in this paper. 

5.2 NPIV as a Collection of Canonical Linear IV Problems 

Consider the NPIV model (6) of Example 1. The structural function hP ;cdr satisfies the conditional moment restriction (8). Under the conditions imposed throughout this paper, the linear operator 

EP : L2(PX ) −→ L2(PZ ), EP h = EP {h(X) | Z = ·} 

is compact. Thus, there exists a singular value decomposition {(σP ;i, u P ;i, v P ;i) : i ∈ I+(P ), σ P ;i > 0, u P ;i ∈

L2(PX ), v P ;i ∈ L2(PZ )} where the set I+(P ) is a countable set of indexes, {uP ;i : i ∈ I+(P )} is an orthonor-mal basis of N (EP )⊥, and {vP ;i : i ∈ I+(P )} is an orthonormal basis of N (E∗ 

> P

)⊥ satisfying 

EP uP ;i = σP ;ivP ;i, E∗ 

> P

vP ;i = σP ;iuP ;i. (32) The singular functions uP ;i have an immediate IV interpretation. Specifically, define the ith canonical endogenous regressor by UP ;i := uP ;i(X) and the corresponding canonical instrument by VP ;i := vP ;i(Z). Equation (32) implies EP (UP ;i | Z) = σP ;iVP ;i. Moreover, orthonormality gives EP {VP ;iUP ;j } = σP ;j I(i =

j). Thus, in the singular coordinates, the population first stage is diagonal. The quantity σP ;i is the first-stage strength of the canonical endogenous regressor UP ;i relative to its canonical instrument VP ;i. A small 

σP ;i therefore corresponds to a weakly instrumented direction. Now the structural function hP ;cdr can be uniquely decomposed as 

hP ;cdr = h† 

> P

+ hP ;cdr; ⊥

where h†

The last two rows have no counterpart in the scalar model (30), in which the structural coefficient is itself the target. As we discuss below, the behavior of the target weights is what distinguishes the consequences of weak instruments in the present setting from those in the scalar model. When X and Z are continuously distributed, and the conditional expectation operator EP has infinite rank, then its positive singular values can be arranged so that σP ;i ↓ 0 as i → ∞ . Hence, the NPIV problem contains an infinite sequence of canonical endogenous regressors whose corresponding instruments become arbitrarily weak. If instead EP has finite rank, there are only finitely many positive singular values; any remaining directions in L2(PX ) belong to N (EP ) and are therefore completely uninstrumented rather than weakly instrumented.

The preceding representation also helps clarify the distinction between identification of the structural function and identification of the target functional. Specifically, as argued above, whenever N (EP )̸ = {0}, the 44 coefficients θ0

{vP ;i : i ≥ 1} is an orthonormal sequence and qP ∈ L2(PZ ), the scalar sequence ( qP ;i)i≥1 is square summable. Hence qP ;i → 0 as i → ∞ . It follows that ψP ;i/σ P ;i = qP ;i → 0 as i → ∞ . Equivalently, 

ψP ;i = o(σP ;i) as i → ∞ .

Thus, at a fixed law P , the range condition forces the target weights to vanish faster than the corre-sponding singular values along the sequence of increasingly weak canonical directions. If EP has finite rank, there are only finitely many positive singular values, so there is no corresponding i → ∞ statement. This canonical-IV representation helps interpret the different statistical models studied in Section 3 and Section 4 and the consequences for inference about the target parameter, as we shall see next. 

5.4 The Baseline Model and the Analogue of Very Weak Instruments 

Consider first the baseline model P. As we argue below, for local inference this model shares important features with the scalar very weak instrument model (30), which places no restrictions on either the structural coefficient θP = δP /σ P or the first-stage coefficient σP . The precise nature of this analogy differs, however, between the finite-discrete and continuous settings, as we now discuss. 

Finite-discrete X and Z. When X and Z are finite discrete, there are only finitely many canonical directions at any fixed law P .45 If EP0 has full rank, none of its singular values is zero, so its smallest singular value is strictly positive. Moreover, since singular values vary continuously with the finite-dimensional matrix representing EP , this smallest singular value remains bounded away from zero for all laws sufficiently close to P0. Hence a canonical first stage cannot become arbitrarily weak in a sufficiently small neighborhood of such a law. Thus, locally at P0, the parameter φ(P ) behaves as in the very-strong IV model that places a uniform lower bound on 

|σP |.In contrast, the discontinuity result in Lemma 3 concerns a rank-deficient law P0. Suppose, as in that lemma, that both EP0 and E∗ 

> P0

have nontrivial null spaces. The proof selects null directions u0 

> P0

∈ N (EP0 )and v0 

> P0

∈ N (E∗ 

> P0

). At P0, these directions correspond to a zero first-stage strength. In addition, the range conditions imply that the corresponding target weight and reduced-form coefficient are both zero: 

⟨ψ, u 0 

> P0

⟩L2(PX;0 ) = 0 because ψ ∈ R(E∗ 

> P0

), and ⟨rP0 , v 0 

> P0

⟩L2(PZ;0 ) = 0 because rP0 ∈ R(EP0 ). The proof then constructs nearby laws Pj → P0 for which a canonical direction approaching these null directions acquires a strictly positive singular value σPj ;i satisfying σPj ;i → 0 for a fixed index i. Along the constructed sequence, the corresponding reduced-form coefficient and target weight also converge to zero. However, the reduced-form coefficient can converge to zero more slowly than the singular value, so that the structural coefficient 

θPj ;i = δPj ;i/σ Pj ;i diverges. Moreover, it can diverge faster than the target weight ψPj ;i converges to zero, causing the contribution ψPj ;iθPj ;i, and hence the target itself, to diverge. The analogy with the scalar very-weak-IV problem is therefore quite direct in the finite-discrete case. The null direction at P0 corresponds to σP0 = 0, and nearby laws Pj correspond to scalar IV laws for which 

σPj > 0 but σPj → 0 in such a way that δPj /σ Pj converges to ∞. There is, however, a subtle difference. In the scalar problem, σP0 = 0 leaves the structural coefficient θP0 unidentified. In the NPIV problem, the structural coefficient along the null direction is likewise unidentified, but the target φ(P0) remains identified because it places zero weight on that direction. 

Continuously distributed X and Z. The situation is different when X and Z are continuously dis-tributed because L2(PX ) and L2(PZ ) are infinite dimensional, while EP is compact under the conditions imposed in this paper. Suppose first that EP0 has infinite rank. Its positive singular values can then be indexed so that σP0;i ↓ 0as i → ∞ . Thus, unlike in the finite-discrete case, arbitrarily weak canonical instruments already exist at the single fixed law P0. This remains true even when EP0 is injective, in which case every canonical structural coefficient is identified and there are no null directions: there are nevertheless identified directions whose first-stage strengths are arbitrarily close to zero. The proof of the discontinuity result of Theorem 1 constructs laws Pj → P0 by perturbing a singular 46 value lying increasingly far into the singular tail as j grows. Thus the canonical direction itself changes with j: one may think of the construction as selecting indices ij → ∞ , for which the associated first-stage strengths are increasingly small, and then perturbing the law in those increasingly weak directions. The perturbations can be made arbitrarily small in Hellinger distance because they occur farther and farther into the tail, while the corresponding singular value can be made sufficiently small relative to the reduced-form coefficients and target weights that the contribution to φ(Pj ) diverges. Hence, in the continuous infinite-rank case, the counterpart of the scalar sequence σPj → 0 is not necessarily a single canonical first-stage coefficient becoming weak as P changes. Rather, it is the existence of a sequence of law-direction pairs ( Pj , i j ) for which σPj ;ij → 0, with increasingly weak directions being selected farther into the singular tail. If EP0 instead has finite rank, there are only finitely many positive singular values, but there are null directions in the infinite-dimensional domain. In that case, the source of arbitrarily weak directions is closer to the finite-discrete rank-deficient case: nearby laws can turn directions that are null, and hence completely uninstrumented, at or near the limiting law into directions with arbitrarily small positive singular values. Thus the continuous case does not require an actual zero singular value at P0 for the discontinuity phenomenon. When EP0 has infinite rank, compactness itself supplies positive singular values arbitrarily close to zero; when it has finite rank, null directions are available instead. This is why Theorem 1 does not require the nontrivial-null-space conditions that appear in Lemma 3. Whether in the rank-deficient discrete case or in the continuous case, the baseline model P permits sequences of law-direction pairs for which the canonical first-stage strength tends to zero without a uniform restriction preventing the associated structural coefficient δP ;i/σ P ;i from becoming arbitrarily large. This is the precise sense in which the discontinuity results are analogous to the very-weak-instrument phenomenon in the scalar IV model. 

5.5 Bounded Structural Coefficients and Continuity 

Now consider the model Ph†

The restriction is instead that the reduced-form coefficient must vanish at least proportionately to the 47 first-stage strength, so that δP ;i/σ P ;i cannot diverge. In the language of the scalar model (30), the model 

Ph†

This explains why the uniform bound restores continuity when P 7 → αP and P 7 → rP are also continuous at P0. Consider again a canonical direction uP ;i that converges to a null direction at P0 as P → P0. Because 

ψ ∈ N (EP0 )⊥, its target weight ψP ;i converges to zero, while its structural coefficient θP ;i remains bounded. Their product, which is the contribution of this direction to φ(P ), therefore converges to zero. In the baseline model, by contrast, the structural coefficient may diverge quickly enough that this contribution diverges. Canonical directions whose singular values are bounded away from zero near P0 are locally well behaved, and contribute continuously to φ(P ). The same intuition extends to continuous X, Z , where the uniform norm bound additionally controls the contribution of the infinitely many weak directions in the singular tail; see the proof of Theorem 3. 

5.6 Weak-Instrument Asymptotics and Failure of Pathwise Differentiability 

Although bounded structural coefficients rule out the analogue of very weak instruments, they do not rule out the analogue of weak-instrument asymptotics. In the scalar model, weak-instrument asymptotics produce a structural coefficient that is a ratio of two quantities local to zero at rate 1 /√n, and which therefore cannot be consistently estimated. In NPIV, the same ratio can arise along a canonical direction, but it is multiplied by a target weight that is itself local to zero at rate 1 /√n. Consequently, the contribution of such a direction to the target vanishes, so that the target remains consistently estimable along these local alternatives, and the weak-instrument instability instead manifests as failure of pathwise differentiability. The construction in the proof of Theorem 5 exhibits this phenomenon at laws P0 where both inverse problems admit multiple solutions, whether X, Z are finite discrete or continuous. As in the proof of Lemma 3, it selects null directions u0 

> P0

∈ N (EP0 ) and v0 

> P0

∈ N (E∗ 

> P0

), which at P0 have zero first-stage strength, zero target weight, and zero reduced-form coefficient. It then constructs a family of QMD at P0 submodels 

{Pt,η : t ∈ [0 , δ η )} of Ph† 

> Pbound

, indexed by a scalar η, along which a pair of canonical directions approaching (u0 

> P0

, v 0 

> P0

) acquires a strictly positive first-stage strength, with the orders of the relevant quantities given by 48 Canonical quantity Order along Pt,η Order under P1/√n,η

Contribution to φ t/η 1/(η√n)Under the local alternative P1/√n,η , the first-stage and reduced-form coefficients are both local to zero at rate 1 /√n, while the structural coefficient remains of constant order 1 /η : this is precisely the configuration of the weak-instrument asymptotics of Staiger and Stock, 1997. Because the structural coefficient remains bounded, the construction respects the uniform norm bound. The contribution of this direction to the target vanishes, so that φ(Pt,η ) ≈ φ(P0)+ t/η is continuous along each submodel. However, its derivative at t = 0 is approximately 1 /η , whereas the score of the submodel, 

sη = s∗ + η¯s for s∗, ¯s ∈ L20(P0) not depending on η, is affine in η. Were φ pathwise differentiable, its derivative along this family would be a bounded linear functional of sη , and hence also affine in η. The term 1 /η therefore rules out pathwise differentiability. The consequence for inference is the local asymptotic bias described following Theorem 5: under P1/√n,η , no asymptotically linear estimator is √n-unbiased for all η, and Wald-type confidence intervals under-cover (Lemma 2). Thus, the same 1 /η term that renders the structural coefficient inconsistently estimable in the scalar weak-instrument problem appears here as non-vanishing √n-scaled bias of every asymptotically linear estimator of the target. 

Pathwise differentiability when the primal solution is unique. The existence of a fixed null direction

> P0
