> Pbound

under mild regularity conditions (Theorem 6 part (a)). When X, Z are finite discrete, injectivity of EP0 also bounds the smallest singular value away from zero locally, so that P0 is locally analogous to a strong-instrument setting. When X, Z are continuous, arbitrarily weak directions remain, but each has strictly positive first-stage strength σP0;i at P0. Perturbing such a direction as above, with first-stage strength σP0;i + ηt and reduced-form coefficient and target weight perturbed by terms of order t, changes its contribution to φ by a term whose quadratic part, of order 

t2/(σP0;i + ηt ), has zero derivative at t = 0. The weak-instrument term 1 /η therefore does not arise; the proof of Theorem 6 controls the infinitely many such directions simultaneously. 49 5.7 The βh-Source Condition and Effective Strong Identification of the Target

5.8 Implications of the Weak-Instrument Connection for X, Z Finite Discrete 

As discussed following Lemma 3, when X, Z are discrete, the laws at which there exist multiple solutions to both the primal and dual inverse problems form a set of measure zero. Nevertheless, the connection between Theorem 5 and the setting of weak instrument asymptotics gives intuition for why the present results are still actionable. In the setting of linear IV, the laws at which pathological behavior occurs ( σP = 0) are Lebesgue measure zero. Nevertheless, when σP is small relative to the sample size, i.e. of order 1 /√n, weak-instrument asymptotics has been demonstrated to give a better finite sample approximation (Andrews, Stock, and Sun, 2019). Taking the same view, in the setting of proximal causal inference, discrete proxies may be weak for the corresponding discrete unmeasured confounder, and in the setting of shadow variables, the shadow variable may be only weakly associated with the outcome subject to missingness, even when it is discrete. If each of σP ;i, ⟨αP , u P ;i⟩L2(PX ), ⟨rP , v P ;i⟩L2(PZ ) is of order 1 /√n (i.e. each is upper and lower bounded by approximately 1 /√n), Wald-type confidence intervals are likely to undercover in finite samples (see Section 7 for an illustration), and alternative interval estimates that account for the weak instruments/proxies/shadow variables may outperform them. See Smucler, Lanni, and Bonet, 2026 for a brief discussion of a related point, who argue that inverting a score test provides better finite sample coverage under weak-instrument asymptotics when X, Z are binary. Similarly, it may prove fruitful to develop alternative confidence interval estimators that are locally uniformly honest under the model Ph†  

> Pbound

at laws P0 with multiple solutions to both inverse problems, even when X, Z are discrete. We leave such constructions to future work. 51 6 Relation to Results Showing Neyman Orthogonality 

The present results are compatible with standard results deriving asymptotically linear estimators for φ using the estimating equation (4) at P0 where the parameter P 7 → φ(P ) is Hellinger discontinuous (Bennett et al., 2023b; Olivas-Martinez and Rotnitzky, 2025). As discussed in Section 2.4.1, pointwise asymptotic consistency of estimators and validity of confidence intervals are strictly weaker than locally uniform consistency of estimators and validity of confidence intervals. Inference can still be performed using standard pointwise asymptotic theory in this setting. However, in light of the finite sample results in Lemma 1 applied at the laws described in Section 3.1, the pointwise asymptotic approximation may be poor for every n: at each sample size there are laws in P that the data cannot distinguish from P0 at which the approximation is arbitrarily poor. Additionally, because the parameter is not pathwise differentiable, all asymptotically linear estimators are irregular (Lemma 2), and semiparametric efficiency theory cannot be used to select between candidate asymptotically linear estimators. This work emphasizes an important distinction between Neyman-orthogonality, the basis under which the estimating equation (4) is justified, and pathwise differentiability. The two concern different perturba-tions: Neyman-orthogonality controls sensitivity to perturbations of the nuisance functions, while pathwise differentiability concerns sensitivity to perturbations of the law itself. Viewing the estimating function (4) as the mapping 

R × L2(PX,D ) × L2(PZ,D ) → L2(P ), (φ, h, q ) 7 → φ1 

> P

(·; h, q, φ ),

Neyman orthogonality is a statement about the composition of this map with the expectation EP : it states that ( h, q ) 7 → EP [φ1 

> P

(O; h, q, φ (P ))] has vanishing Gateaux derivative at ( hP , q P ), where the nuisance space carries its L2 topology. On the other hand, pathwise differentiability considers differentiability relative to the Hellinger topology, or rather relative to the Hellinger differentiable one dimensional paths in P(P0, P). The latter topology is in some sense more natural for deriving statistical results, at least those related to asymptotic optimality, and is the one on which semiparametric efficiency theory is built (Bickel et al., 1998; Vaart, 1998). Recent work has attempted to unify these related concepts; see for example Chen, Kennedy, and Balakrishnan, 2026, who show that under regularity conditions and correct choice of nuisance parameters, pathwise differentiability and Neyman-orthogonality imply each other. The setting of this paper shows that this equivalence does not always hold. The hypothesis that fails here is a smoothness requirement on the nuisance parameters. Letting η(P )denote the nuisance parameters and β(P ) the target of inference, that work requires the mapping t 7 → η(Pt)52 be differentiable at t = 0 whenever {Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} ⊆ P is a QMD at P0 submodel. In the setting of this paper, η(P ) := ( h† 

> P

, q † 

> P

) and β(P ) = φ(P ). As discussed in Section 3.2, the mapping t 7 → (h† 

> Pt

, q † 

> Pt

) fails to be differentiable along some {Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} ∈ P(P0, P). Because the requirement is imposed at every such submodel, a single submodel along which differentiability fails suffices to place the present setting outside the scope of that equivalence.

We now perform a small simulation study based on the above example. We use R (R Core Team, 2026) and the R package SimEngine (Kenny and Wolock, 2024) to run our simulations. We generate data at sample sizes n = 200 , 2000 , 20000 , 200000. For each n and each data generating process, we simulate 1000 replicates. Table 1 reports the bias, the √n-scaled bias, and the coverage of the Wald-type confidence intervals for the three data generating processes. φ(P0.1/√n), which varies with both n and the sampling process, and which we evaluate exactly rather than by simulation. For reference, φ(P0) = 13 /3, and under the fixed alternative the estimand equals φ(P0) at every n. With 1000 replicates, the Monte Carlo standard error of the coverage estimates is at most 1.6 percentage points. Under the fixed alternative, the estimator displays low bias and nominal coverage, as standard pointwise asymptotic theory predicts. Under the irregular alternative, the estimator is consistent but has a √n-scaled bias that is essentially constant across sample sizes, as predicted by Lemma 2. This reflects the fact that the limit distributions of irregular estimators depend on the score of the local alternative path. Here, this dependence prevents the limit distribution under the irregular alternative from having mean zero. The large √n-scaled bias in turn leads to poor coverage of the Wald-type confidence intervals under the 56 Table 1: Bias, scaled bias, 95% confidence interval coverage, and mean confidence interval width of the Tikhonov-regularized IV estimator under each data-generating process.

As expected from the contiguity argument above, under the discontinuous alternative, the bias, and hence the √n-scaled bias, diverges to infinity, and the coverage is 0% for all sample sizes. The poor asymptotic behavior under the two local alternatives cannot be captured in a pointwise asymptotic analysis, as seen by the low bias and nominal coverage of the estimator under the fixed alternative data generating process. 

# 8 Discussion 

We have demonstrated that, under minimal conditions that suffice for the existence of a Neyman orthogonal estimating equation and further regularity, parameters defined as continuous linear functionals of solutions to ill-posed inverse problems are not Hellinger continuous at laws where both inverse problems are ill-posed, which is typical when X and Z are continuous. Locally uniform inference is impossible over this model, even in finite samples: for every n, no estimator attains any finite accuracy uniformly over Hellinger neighborhoods of P0, and every confidence interval with nominal coverage locally uniformly has infinite expected diameter. Asymptotically, there do not exist locally uniformly consistent estimators, and all asymptotically locally uniformly honest confidence intervals have diameters that are not bounded in probability under P0. These results do not conflict with recent work constructing pointwise asymptotically linear estimators, as pointwise inference is strictly weaker than locally uniform inference. Under stronger assumptions, including a uniform 57 bound over the model on the magnitude of the minimum norm solution to one of the inverse problems, Hellinger continuity is restored. However, the parameter may still fail to be pathwise differentiable over this model. Without additional assumptions, and under regularity conditions, pathwise differentiability fails at laws P0 such that the inverse problem with uniformly bounded solutions does not admit a unique solution when both inverse problems are ill-posed. Consequently, at such laws, all asymptotically linear estimators are irregular, and exhibit non-vanishing √n-scaled bias and asymptotic undercoverage of Wald-type intervals under some local alternative data generating processes. On the other hand, if this solution is unique, and further regularity holds, the parameter is pathwise differentiable. Pathwise differentiability also holds under the stronger assumption that the β-source representer of one of the two solutions admits a bound uniformly over the model, whether or not the corresponding inverse problem admits a unique solution at P0.Whether a uniform norm bound on the minimum norm solution to one of the inverse problems is available depends on the substantive problem. In the shadow variable model, a uniform bound on the dual solution follows from a uniform bound on the density ratio between the outcome distribution in the nonrespondents and the respondents, and so functions as a positivity-like overlap condition. In the correctly specified additive NPIV model it follows from the structural interpretation of the primal solution: the causal dose response curve solves the primal inverse problem, so uniformly bounded counterfactual outcomes bound the minimum norm solution directly. Under mild violations of the exclusion restriction this argument breaks down, and the assumption appears to be a technical stability condition not justified based on any counterfactual interpre-tation. In proximal causal inference the situation is unresolved. A correctly specified proximal model with bounded outcomes does bound the target parameter, so our discontinuity constructions must violate that model, but whether the model restricts the bridge functions enough to restore Hellinger continuity remains an open question. We are unaware of any general substantive assumption that leads to a uniform norm bound on the β-source representer. We have also investigated the maximal tangent space of various statistical models. We have shown that the assumptions encoded in the baseline model P do not restrict the maximal tangent space of the statistical model when the dimensions of the spaces L2(PX,D ) and L2(PZ,D ) are equal. When the inverse problems are finite dimensional and these dimensions differ, the existence of solutions to both equations is potentially substantive whenever the operators defining them are full rank, a condition that fails only on a set of laws of measure zero. In contrast to the baseline model, under the conditions that suffice for pathwise differentiability, we are able to derive a superset of the tangent space, proving it is no larger than the space derived by Severini and Tripathi, 2012 and Chen and Santos, 2018 under stronger assumptions. When the primal solution is uniformly norm bounded and admits a unique solution at P0, or when its β-source representer is uniformly norm bounded, this bound is substantive under mild conditions when there exist 58 multiple solutions to the dual inverse problem, in that the tangent space is strictly smaller than that of a locally non-parametric model. Our results mirror in many respects the classical results on weak instruments in the linear instrumental variables problem. The results under the baseline model resemble impossibility results in the unrestricted linear IV problem (Gleser and Hwang, 1987; Dufour, 1997), the results under a uniform norm bound on the primal solution when that solution is not unique at P0 are related to the framing of weak-instrument asymptotics (Staiger and Stock, 1997), and the uniform bound on the norm of the β-source representer can be viewed as a type of strong instrument assumption. Given this relation, it may be fruitful to adapt results for performing valid inference in the linear IV problem (Andrews, Stock, and Sun, 2019) to the present setting, a task we leave to future work. These results also separate two notions of differentiability that recent work has sought to unify. Neyman-orthogonality of the estimating equation (4) concerns differentiability with respect to the L2 topology on the nuisance functions, while pathwise differentiability concerns differentiability along Hellinger differentiable paths through the model. Chen, Kennedy, and Balakrishnan, 2026 show that the two coincide under condi-tions requiring the nuisance functions to vary smoothly along quadratic mean differentiable submodels. The parameters studied here satisfy the former without satisfying the latter, precisely because that smoothness fails. The practical consequence is visible in Section 7: Wald intervals constructed from the influence function of (4) undercover under local alternatives, potentially quite severely, as predicted by Lemma 2.

# S.1 Parameter Smoothness and Implications for Inference 

S.1.1 Implications of Hellinger Discontinuity 

Here, we give an extended discussion of the results in Section 2.4.1. As described there, the finite-sample impossibility results that we shall derive below are in the same spirit of classical results on nonexistence of ”honest” inference about locally unstable functionals. The earliest example is due to Bahadur and Savage, 1956, who showed that, over large nonparametric classes, effective distribution-free inference for the mean is impossible. Subsequent work developed this idea in several directions: Donoho, 1988 and Donoho and Liu, 1991 related nonparametric inference to moduli of continuity, defined in Section 2.4.1; Gleser and Hwang, 1987 showed that uniform confidence sets in errors-in-variables and related models cannot have finite expected diameter; Low, 1997 and Cai and Low, 2004 gave lower bounds on the expected length of honest nonparametric confidence intervals in terms of moduli of continuity; and Pfanzagl, 1998 provided a general formulation of nonexistence results for confidence procedures for discontinuous functionals. Closely related impossibility results also appear in econometrics in the work of Dufour, 1997 and P¨ otscher, 2002. 64 We now prove Lemma 1, using the local modulus of continuity ωP0 , the notation P n, Cn, and |Cn|, and the radius rn,τ defined in (11), all introduced in Section 2.4.1. We additionally let dTV denote the usual total variation distance dTV (P, Q ) := sup A |P (A) − Q(A)|. We build on the following finite-sample bounds, which follow from elementary two-point testing arguments. 

Proposition S.1 (A finite-sample two-point lower bound) . Fix two laws P0 and P1 in M. Write ∆ := 

|ϕ(P1) − ϕ(P0)|. Suppose that Cn has coverage at least 1 − α at both P0 and P1, that is, 

P n 

> 0

{ϕ(P0) ∈ Cn} ≥ 1 − α, P n 

> 1

{ϕ(P1) ∈ Cn} ≥ 1 − α. 

Then

η ↓ 0. 66 S.1.2 Implications of Failure of Pathwise Differentiability 

Here we give a more in depth discussion of Section 2.4.2. Pathwise differentiability, formally defined in Section 2.3, is a strictly stronger smoothness condition than Hellinger continuity. 

Remark S.1. To see that failure of Hellinger continuity implies failure of pathwise differentiability, note that failure of Hellinger continuity implies the existence of a sequence {Pj }∞ 

> j=1

⊆ M and constant ϵ > 0

for which H(Pj , P 0) → 0 but where |ϕ(Pj ) − ϕ(P0)| > ϵ for each j. Passing to a subsequence {ji}∞ 

> i=1

with 

H(Pji , P 0) ≤ i−2, we have H(Pj⌊1/t ⌋ , P 0) ≤ ⌊ 1/t ⌋−2 = o(t) as t ↓ 0. Then, {Pj⌊1/t ⌋ : t ∈ [0 , 1) } ⊆ M ,where we take the convention that Pj⌊1/0⌋ = P0, is QMD at P0 with score 0, but |ϕ(Pj⌊1/t ⌋ ) − ϕ(P0)|/t > ϵ/t 

diverges to infinity as t ↓ 0, so that ϕ is not pathwise differentiable at P0. The converse fails in general; see Section 4.2 for laws and models at which φ is Hellinger continuous but not pathwise differentiable. 

We now prove Lemma 2, using the notation and setting of Section 2.4.2. 

Proof of Lemma 2. We first analyze the behavior of ̂ ϕn in case 1). Define the map D : Θ 7 → R via

Since Cz is fixed and infinitely differentiable on (0 , 1) 2, and the conditional margins are polynomial in t,the cell probabilities of ( W, X )|Z = z are continuously differentiable in t for each z. Furthermore, because 

|ρ(z)| < 1, the Gaussian copula assigns positive mass to every cell, so these cell probabilities are bounded away from zero for t small enough. Hence, {P Irreg t : t ∈ [0 , δ )} and {P Discont t : t ∈ [0 , δ )} are both QMD submodels of P at P0.Our choice of copula Cz is implicitly given in steps 2 and 3 of the sampling procedure we now outline. 69 For generic (conditional) CDFs PZ of Z, PX|Z of X|Z, and PW |Z of W |Z generate an observation as follows: (a) Sample Z ∼ PZ .(b) Sample

Here we argue that under mild conditions on P0, the Riesz representers P 7 → αP , P 7 → rP are both Hellinger continuous and pathwise differentiable at P0 in the model PRiesz defined in (22), and hence also relative to any submodel of PRiesz , including P.

Example 1 (continued) . Recall that P 7 → αP = ψ which does not depend on P , and so is pathwise dif-ferentiable with local parameter ˙α[s] = 0. On the other hand, recall that rP := EP [W |Z = ·], and that the support of W is bounded. Then, sup P ∈P Riesz ess sup z EP [W 2|Z = z] < ∞. Consequently, Example 5 of Luedtke and Chung, 2024 shows that P 7 → rP is pathwise differentiable at P in model PRiesz , and so also for any submodel of PRiesz . Example 5 of Luedtke and Chung, 2024 further shows that P 7 → rP is Lipschitz with respect to the Hellinger distance over PRiesz , and so is Hellinger continuous at P0 over PRiesz and any submodel of it. As αP = ψ does not depend on P , P 7 → αP is trivially Hellinger continuous. 

Example 2 (continued) . Recall that rP := EP [W |Z = ·, D = ·], which is pathwise differentiable by the same reasoning as rP in Example 1. On the other hand, αP (X, D ) := 2D−1  

> P(D|X)

. P (D|X) = DE P [D|X] + (1 −

D)EP [1 − D|X], which is a linear combination of two regression functions, each pathwise differentiable, and so P (D|X) is pathwise differentiable at P in PRiesz . If we additionally assume that P0(D|X) > δ for some 

δ > 0, then by Assumption 5 P0(D|X) > δ ′ for some δ′ > 0. Consequently, the chain rule shows that 

P 7 → 2D−1  

> P(D|X)

is pathwise differentiable at P in the model PRiesz .For Hellinger continuity, P 7 → rP is Lipschitz with respect to the Hellinger distance over PRiesz by the same reasoning as in Example 1, and similarly for the regression functions EP [D|X] and EP [1 − D|X], so that P 7 → P (D|X) is also Lipschitz with respect to the Hellinger distance. Furthermore, because D is binary and the density of every P ∈ P Riesz is bounded above and below on a common rectangle by Assumption 5, there exists δ′ > 0 such that P (D = d|X) > δ ′ P -a.s. for every P ∈ P Riesz and d ∈ { 0, 1}. As x 7 → 1/x is Lipschitz on [ δ′, 1] and |2D − 1| = 1, P 7 → αP is Lipschitz with respect to the Hellinger distance over PRiesz ,and so is Hellinger continuous at P0 over PRiesz and any submodel of it. 

Example 3 (continued) . Recall that rP = DZ , which does not depend on P and as such is pathwise differ-entiable with local parameter ˙ r[s] = 0. On the other hand, if P0(D = 1 |X) > δ > 0 for some δ > 0, the same reasoning as αP in Example 2 shows that P 7 → αP is pathwise differentiable at P in the model PRiesz . The same reasoning as in Example 2 also shows that P 7 → αP is Lipschitz with respect to the Hellinger distance over PRiesz , and as rP does not depend on P , both Riesz representer maps are Hellinger continuous at P0

over PRiesz and any submodel of it. 78 S.4 Proofs of Main Text Results

S.4.1 Technical Lemmas 

We begin by proving some technical lemmas. In what follows, given a law P for ( W, X, Z, D ) with density 

p with respect to some dominating measure, we write PV and pV for the corresponding law and density of a subvector V of ( W, X, Z, D ). Because D is finite discrete, the operators EP and E∗ 

> P

act blockwise in d, i.e. (EP h)( ·, d ) = EP ;dh(·, d ). When we describe such operators as integral operators with a kernel in ( x, z, d ), or refer to their Hilbert–Schmidt norms, we mean that for each d the block is an integral operator with the corresponding kernel in ( x, z ). As D takes finitely many values, a property of the Hilbert–Schmidt norms 79 that holds for every block, such as finiteness or convergence, holds for the operator as a whole.

In what follows C∞(Rd) is the class of real valued infinitely differentiable functions on Rd and C∞ 

> c

(Rd) is the subclass of C∞(Rd) of functions that take value 0 outside a compact set. For A ⊆ Rd open and bounded, we define C∞(A) the space of infinitely differentiable functions with domain A, and C∞(A) the space of infinitely differentiable functions whose derivatives extend continuously to A. The class L1

> loc

(λ) is the class of locally integrable functions, that is, the class of real valued functions f on Rd such that ∫ 

> K

|f (x)|dλ (x) < ∞

for any compact set K, where λ is the Lebesgue measure on Rd and the dimension d is understood according to the context. Furthermore, for g ∈ C∞ 

> c

(Rd) and f ∈ L1

> loc

(λ), g ∗ f is the convolution of g with f , i.e. (g ∗ f )( y) := 

∫

g(y − y′)f (y′)dy ′

and it holds that g ∗ f ∈ C∞ 

> c

(Rd).

The next lemma will invoke the following results: 82 (a) (Special case of the Approximate Identity Theorem (Folland, 2013, Theorem 8.14.)) Suppose that 

ρ is a non-negative real-valued function on Rd supported on B(0 , 1) := {y ∈ Rd : ∥y∥ ≤ 1} with 

∫ ρ(x)dλ (x) = 1, and for each t > 0 let ρt(y) := t−dρ(y/t ), so that ρt is supported on B(0 , t ) and 

∫ ρt(x)dλ (x) = 1, where λ is the Lebesgue measure on Rd. Let q ∈ [1 , ∞). Then, if f ∈ Lq (λ) it holds that ∥ρt ∗ f − f ∥Lq (λ) → 0 as t → 0. (b) (Young’s convolution inequality). Let 1 ≤ q ≤ ∞ . Suppose f ∈ L1(Rd) and g ∈ Lq (Rd). Then the convolution f ∗ g belongs to Lq (Rd), and ∥f ∗ g∥Lq ≤ ∥ f ∥L1 ∥g∥Lq .

We note that this lemma will be invoked in the proofs of Theorem 1, Theorem 2 to construct a sequence of laws Pj → P that each admit continuously differentiable versions of their densities. These well behaved 

Pj ’s will then be further perturbed to construct the sequences and submodels used in those results. A similar construction appears in Canay, Santos, and Shaikh, 2013 to prove that completeness is untestable when X, Z 

are continuous.

We next invoke a classical result of Birman and Solomyak on the decay of singular values of integral operators with Sobolev kernels. Recall that Ω = Ω X × ΩZ ⊂ Rd, where d = ℓX + ℓZ , is a bounded rectangle, and in particular a bounded Lipschitz domain. The Birman–Solomyak theorem states (Griebel and Li, 2018, Birman and Solomyak, 1977 Section 2, Proposition 2.1) that if the kernel of an integral operator belongs to W s, 2(Ω), then the singular values of the operator decay polynomially at a rate determined by the smoothness s and the ambient dimension d. More precisely, if (T f )( z) = 

∫

> ΩX

k(x, z )f (x) dλ X (x),

with k ∈ W s, 2(Ω), then the singular values of T decay as σi(T ) = O(i−s/d ). This result applies to T

with kernel k, which acts between the Lebesgue spaces L2(λX ) and L2(λZ ). The conditional expectation operator factors as EP = JZ T M pX , where MpX : L2(PX ) → L2(λX ) is multiplication by pX and JZ :

L2(λZ ) → L2(PZ ) is the identity map. Both are bounded because pX and pZ are bounded above. Since 

σi(JZ T M pX ) ≤ ∥ JZ ∥ ∥ MpX ∥ σi(T ) for every i, this yields 

σi = O(i−s/d ).

Lemma S.6. Suppose that P0 satisfies Assumption 1, Assumption 5, and that m, ˜m, P 0 satisfy Assumption 6. Let P be a probability law for O = ( W, X, Z, D ) dominated by the same product measure λ as P0, and suppose that P and P0 are supported on the same bounded rectangle Ω. Let p and p0 denote the corresponding densities with respect to λ. Assume that for some constant C > 1,

C−1 < p (o) < C λ-a.e. on the common support. Then P and P0 are mutually absolutely continuous. Moreover, P satisfies Assumption 1. Moreover, the Riesz representers at P admit essentially bounded versions: 

∥αP ∥L∞(PX,D ) < ∞, ∥rP ∥L∞(PZ,D ) < ∞.

Proof of Lemma S.6. The density bounds on p0 and p imply that P and P0 are mutually absolutely contin-uous since p and p0 are strictly positive and finite λ-a.e. on the common support. We prove the claim for 

αP . The argument for rP is identical. By Assumption 6, there exists LP < ∞ such that, for every h ∈ L1(PX,D ;0 ), 

∥ ˜m(W, X, D ; h)∥L1(PW,X,D ;0 ) ≤ LP ∥h∥L1(PX,D ;0 ).

Since p0 and p are both bounded above and below by positive constants on the same bounded rectangle, the marginal densities p0,X,D and pX,D are also bounded above and below by positive constants on their common support. Hence the L1(PX,D ;0 ) and L1(PX,D ) norms are equivalent: there exists A < ∞ such that 

∥h∥L1(PX,D ;0 ) ≤ A∥h∥L1(PX,D )

94 for all measurable h ∈ L1(PX,D ). Similarly, the marginal densities of ( W, X, D ) under P0 and P are bounded above and below by positive constants on their common support. Therefore, for some B < ∞,

pW,X,D (w, x, d )

p0,W,X,D (w, x, d ) ≤ B P0-a.e. Consequently, for every h ∈ L1(PX,D ), 

|EP { ˜m(W, X, D ; h)}| ≤ EP {| ˜m(W, X, D ; h)|} 

= EP0

[ pW,X,D (W, X, D )

p0,W,X,D (W, X, D ) | ˜m(W, X, D ; h)|

]

≤ BE P0 {| ˜m(W, X, D ; h)|} ≤ BL P ∥h∥L1(PX,D ;0 )

≤ BL P A∥h∥L1(PX,D ).

Thus the map 

h 7 → EP { ˜m(W, X, D ; h)}

is a bounded linear functional on L1(PX,D ). By the dual representation of bounded linear functionals on L1, there exists αP ∈ L∞(PX,D ) such that 

EP { ˜m(W, X, D ; h)} = EP {h(X, D )αP (X, D )}

for every h ∈ L1(PX,D ). Since P is a probability measure, L2(PX,D ) ⊂ L1(PX,D ), and the same identity holds for every h ∈ L2(PX,D ). Moreover, 

|EP { ˜m(W, X, D ; h)}| ≤ ∥ αP ∥L∞(PX,D )∥h∥L1(PX,D ) ≤ ∥ αP ∥L∞(PX,D )∥h∥L2(PX,D ).

Hence the functional is also bounded on L2(PX,D ). Since αP ∈ L∞(PX,D ) ⊂ L2(PX,D ), this is precisely the Riesz representer required in Assumption 1. The same argument, replacing ˜m, α P , (X, D ) by m, r P , (Z, D ), shows that ∥rP ∥L∞(PZ,D ) < ∞ and that the second functional in Assumption 1 is bounded on L2(PZ,D ). This proves the lemma.

We conclude this subsection with a differentiability property of the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse. Stan-dard statements of this result assume that the matrix valued mapping is differentiable on a neighborhood of the point in question. Quadratic mean differentiability of a submodel at t = 0 delivers differentiability of the induced matrix valued mappings at the single point t = 0 only, and so we record the pointwise version we require. 

Lemma S.10. Let δ > 0 and let t 7 → A(t) map [0 , δ ) into the set of m × n real matrices. Suppose that (a) A′(0) := lim t→0 t−1{A(t) − A(0) } exists, and (b) rank (A(t)) = rank (A(0)) for each t ∈ [0 , δ ).Then t 7 → A(t)† and t 7 → I − A(t)†A(t) are differentiable at t = 0 , with 

ddt A(t)†

∣∣∣∣t=0 

= − A(0) †A′(0) A(0) † + A(0) †{A(0) †}T A′(0) T {I − A(0) A(0) †}

+ {I − A(0) †A(0) }A′(0) T {A(0) †}T A(0) †,

104 where A† denotes the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse of a matrix A. Here derivatives and limits at t = 0 are understood as one-sided. Proof of Lemma S.10. For A and B two m × n real matrices of equal rank, writing E := B − A, it holds that 

B† − A† = −B†EA † + B†{B†}T ET (I − AA †) + ( I − B†B)ET {A†}T A† (S.15) (Stewart and Sun, 1990 Chapter 3, Lemma 3.7). We apply (S.15) with B := A(t) and A := A(0), which have equal rank by condition (b), so that 

E = A(t) − A(0). Condition (a) implies lim t→0 A(t) = A(0), which together with condition (b) gives lim t→0 A(t)† = A(0) † (Stewart and Sun, 1990 Chapter 3, Corollary 3.10). Dividing (S.15) by t and letting 

t → 0, condition (a) gives t−1E → A′(0), and each of the three terms on the right hand side converges. This gives the claimed derivative. Differentiability of t 7 → I − A(t)†A(t) at t = 0 then follows because both 

t 7 → A(t)† and t 7 → A(t) are differentiable at t = 0.

S.4.2 Proofs for Section 3.1 

Proof of Lemma 3. We begin by giving an informal proof sketch. The proof of part (b) relies on the following facts. First, when N (EP0;d) = {0} or N (E∗

> P0;d

) = {0}, the minimum singular value of EP0;d is separated from zero by a positive constant. Second, with t real-vector valued, given a continuous at t = 0 matrix valued function t 7 → A(t), the singular values are continuous in t, and if A(t) has rank equal to A(0) for t near zero, the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse of A(t) is also continuous at t = 0. Because W, X, Z, D are discrete, 

P can be parameterized by a real vector, namely the cell probabilities P (W = w, X = x, Z = z, D = d). Consequently, for P close enough to P0, continuity of the singular values implies EP ;d is full rank. Then, the mapping P 7 → E †  

> P;d

is continuous at P0, with E†  

> P;d

the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse. W, X, Z, D discrete and the restrictions on P further guarantee the map P 7 → αP , r P is continuous at P0, and so the solution to the primal inverse problem given by the matrix pseudo-inverse is continuous at P0. Consequently, because 

φ(P ) takes the same value at every solution, P 7 → φ(P ) is continuous at P0. Note that this proof relies on 

X, Z finite discrete. When X, Z are continuous, the minimum singular value of EP0;d is not separated from zero, and the Moore-Penrose pseudo inverse is discontinuous. On the other hand, when neither N (EP0;d) = {0} nor N (E∗

> P0;d

) = {0} for some d, the minimum singular value of EP0;d is equal to zero. This guarantees that the mapping P 7 → E †  

> P;d

is discontinuous. The proof of part (a) proceeds by constructing a sequence {Pj }∞ 

> j=1

that exploits this discontinuity by causing the minimum norm solution to diverge to infinity as j → ∞ . We do so by perturbing the singular system of 

EP0;d so that αP0 (·, d ), r P0 (·, d ) place non-zero mass on a pair of singular vectors with singular value equal to zero, then perturbing that singular value to be small but non-zero. These perturbations cause small changes in P , but large changes to the value of h† 

> P

, and consequently to φ(P ). The majority of the argument is devoted to ensuring our perturbations result in a valid probability distribution. In the following, we note that h solves the equation EP h = f if and only if hd := h(·, d ) solves EP ;dhd =108 f (·, d ) for each d, and similarly for E∗ 

> P

Proof of Theorem 1. We begin by providing a sketch of the proof. The core argument is similar to that of the discrete case. Because the minimum eigenvalue of EP ;d is not separated from zero, the mapping P 7 → E † 

> P;d

with E†  

> P;d

the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse is discontinuous. We exploit this discontinuity by perturbing 

P0 to Pj where αPj , r Pj are supported on a pair of singular vectors with small singular value. Perturbing this singular value closer to zero then causes the minimum norm solution h† 

> Pj

, and so φ(Pj ), to diverge. As 

j increases, we do this perturbation further into the tail of the singular system, so that the perturbation has a vanishingly small effect on the Hellinger distance between Pj and P0.As in the discrete case, the majority of the argument is devoted to ensuring our perturbation Pj is a 116 valid distribution. In contrast to the discrete case, this is complicated by the fact that, in general, the uniform norm of the singular functions can grow to infinity as we move further into the tail of the singular system. Then, a small perturbation to the tail of the singular system may cause the kernel of the conditional expectation operator to be negative for some values of x, z . However, as the kernel is a conditional density for the appropriate choice of reference measure, it must be non-negative. We solve this by controlling the uniform norm of these singular vectors by constructing x, z 7 → pX,Z |d;j infinitely differentiable for each j,

d. Lemma S.3 then shows that a truncated singular system of an infinitely differentiable density will still be a valid density, as the tail contributes negligibly in uniform norm. To ensure αPj , rPj are in range of this truncated operator, we add the residuals of αPj and rPj as singular vectors, with singular value small enough to ensure the kernel of the new operator is a valid conditional density. The β-source conditions are also trivially satisfied for every βh, β q as the operator EPj ;d is finite dimensional. We now begin our formal arguments. Write W = ( WC , W D ), where WC collects the components of W

whose reference measure in Assumption 5 is the Lebesgue measure and WD collects those whose reference measure is the counting measure; either may be empty. Correspondingly, λW = λWC ⊗ λWD , where λWC ,

λX , λZ are Lebesgue product measures and λWD , λD are counting measures. All densities are taken with respect to λ, or the appropriate product measure for functions of W, X, Z, D and combinations thereof. We normalize these reference measures as in Remark S.2, so that λW (Supp [W ]) = λX (Supp [X]) = λZ (Supp [Z]) = 

λD (Supp [D]) = 1. Let ˜d ∈ Supp [D; P0] such that αP0 (·, ˜d), r P0 (·, ˜d) are not P0-a.e. constant functions. We first construct a sequence of laws ( Pj )∞ 

> j=1

whose densities are smooth in ( WC , X, Z ) and lie strictly inside the density bounds of Assumption 5. Because ( WD , D ) is supported on a bounded rectangle in a product of copies of N, the set S := Supp [( WD , D ); P0] is finite. Let Ω C denote the open bounded rectangle supporting ( WC , X, Z ) under P0. Proof of Theorem 2. We begin by giving an informal sketch of the proof. The proof is similar to that of Theorem 1. As before, the main technical difficulty arises from ensuring that our proposed path Pt is a valid joint distribution for O. We begin by taking a linear tilt QMD submodel { ˜Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} with uniformly bounded score, and perturb this submodel so it belongs to P but such that the perturbation does not change the score. We construct this perturbation over several steps. First, by Lemma S.2, we have for every t that 134 the conditional density of ( WC , X, Z ) given the discrete variables ( WD , D ) under ˜Pt is arbitrarily close in 

Lq (λ) to a conditional density that is infinitely differentiable on the compact support; recombining with the law of ( WD , D ) gives a law P † 

> t

satisfying the density bounds of Assumption 5. In light of Lemma S.3, we may truncate the singular system of EP †

> t

sufficiently far into the tail to construct a new finite dimensional singular system that corresponds to the conditional expectation operator of a valid joint density for X, Z 

given D = d. We may further perturb this finite dimensional singular system so that α˜P †

> t

, r ˜P †

> t

are in the range of the operator and its adjoint. The β-source conditions are then satisfied for this operator, as it is finite dimensional. Lemma S.1 then gives a full joint distribution Pt for O belonging to the model P.We may make these perturbations sufficiently small so that for each t, H(Pt, ˜Pt) = o(t). Consequently, 

{Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} has the same score as { ˜Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )}. As the score of { ˜Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} was arbitrary beyond belonging to a dense subspace of L20(P0), this proves the result. Let s ∈ L∞(P0) ∩ L20(P0), and define a new density ˜pt(o) := (1 + ts )p0(o). Let ˜Pt be the corresponding distribution. It follows that for 0 < t < ∥s∥−1

> L∞(P0)

, ˜pt is a density and ˜Pt is mutually absolutely continuous with P0. Let 

Lgap := min(ess inf p0 − L−1

> bounded

, L bounded − ess sup p0),

min(1 , ∥s∥−1

> L∞(P0)

) so that for all t < t s,

> bounded

, L bounded − ess sup ˜pt).

Now, because Assumption 5 holds at P0, and rP0 ∈ R(EP0 ), α P0 ∈ R(E∗ 

> P0

) by Assumption 2, we have by Lemma S.4 that 

∥αP0 ∥L∞(PX,D ;0 ) < ∞, ∥rP0 ∥L∞(PZ,D ;0 ) < ∞.

By Lemma S.6, Assumption 1 holds at ˜Pt and α˜Pt

∈ L∞( ˜PX,D ;t), r˜Pt

In what follows, we write W = ( WC , W D ), where WC collects the components of W whose reference measure in Assumption 5 is the Lebesgue measure and WD collects those whose reference measure is the counting measure; either may be empty. Correspondingly, λW = λWC ⊗ λWD , where λWC , λX , λZ are Lebesgue product measures and λWD , λD are counting measures. All densities are taken with respect to λ,or the appropriate product measure for functions of W, X, Z, D and combinations thereof. We normalize these reference measures as in Remark S.2, so that λW (Supp [W ]) = λX (Supp [X]) = λZ (Supp [Z]) = λD (Supp [D]) = 135 1. Now, for each t, we construct a sequence {P †

Proof of Lemma 5. We first provide a sketch of the proofs. part (a) relies on the fact that a differentiable (at 

t = 0) matrix valued mapping t 7 → A(t) with constant rank for t near 0 has differentiable (at t = 0) Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse. Then, both sides of the equation rPt = EPt h† 

> Pt

, with h† 

> Pt

:= E† 

> Pt

rPt , are differentiable in t, giving a restriction on the scores s. We extend slightly the argument of Severini and Tripathi, 2012, noting that the above moment restriction holds for any choice of solution, not just the minimum norm solution, leading to an infinite collection of restrictions on the scores s. The same argument holds for the dual inverse problem, giving yet more restrictions. part (b) uses that when EP0;d are full rank for each d, there exists an ϵ > 0 such that the local model 

Pϵ := {P ∈ P : H2(P, P 0) < ϵ } satisfies the constant rank assumption because the minimum singular value of EP0;d is separated from zero. The maximal tangent space of Pϵ at P0 is equal to the maximal tangent space of P at P0 for every ϵ > 0, giving the result. 

Proof of part (a): 

Let {Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} ⊆ P rank be any one-dimensional QMD submodel with Pt=0 = P0. Let s ∈ L20(P0)142 denote its score. We first show that s satisfies that ˙rP0 [s]( Z, D ) − EP0 [hP0 (X, D )sX|Z,D (X, Z, D )|Z, D ] ∈ R(EP0 )for any hP0 satisfying EP0 hP0 = rP0 . Because X, Z are supported on {1, . . . , K X }, {1, . . . , K Z }, respectively, we may equivalently view EP0;d as a KZ × KX -matrix operating on vectors of length KX , after associating 

L2(PX|d;0 ) with RKX and L2(PZ|d;0 ) with RKZ . I.e., we write f ∈ L2(PX|d;0 ) in vector form as f := (f (1) , . . . , f (KX )) T , and write EP0;d in matrix form as 

EP0;d =

P (X = 1 |Z = KZ , D = d) · · · P (X = KX |Z = KZ , D = d)

 .

Proof of part (b): 

By definition, P0 ∈ P rank and Prank ⊆ P . Consequently, T (P0, Prank ) ⊆ T (P0, P). We now argue 

T (P0, P) ⊆ T (P0, Prank ). Let {Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )} ⊆ P . Because this submodel is QMD, lim t→0 H2(Pt, P 0) = 0, and because X, Z are supported on a rectangle for each d, Pt(X = x|Z = z, D = d) → P0(X = x|Z = z, D =

d) for each x, z, d . Consequently, because KX , K Z < ∞, the matrix representing EPt;d in the canonical bases, with ( z, x ) entry Pt(X = x|Z = z, D = d), converges entrywise, and hence in Frobenius norm, to the matrix representing EP0;d as t → 0. Consequently, the singular values of these matrix representations converge (Stewart and Sun, 1990 Chapter IV Theorem 1.3). On the other hand, because either N (EP0 ) = {0} or 

N (E∗ 

> P0

) = {0}, for each d either N (EP0;d) = {0} or N (E∗

> P0;d

) = {0}, so that EP0;d has rank min( KX , K Z )and the smallest of the min( KX , K Z ) singular values of its matrix representation is strictly positive. Then, for T small enough, these singular values are strictly positive for all t < T . Hence, for t < T ,rank( EPt;d) = min( KX , K Z ) = rank( EP0;d) for each d, the rank of an operator being independent of the bases in which it is represented. Then, {Pt : t ∈ [0 , T )} ⊆ P rank . Consequently, the score of this submodel belongs to T (P0, Prank ), and so T (P0, P) ⊆ T (P0, Prank ). This completes the proof. 145 S.4.5 Proofs for Section 4.1 

In the proof of Theorem 3 we use the following definitions. If T : H 7 → H is a compact, non-negative, and self-adjoint operator on the Hilbert space H, with λ1, λ 2, · · · being its eigenvalues sorted in descending order and u1, u 2, · · · the corresponding eigenfunctions, then spec( T ) := {λi}i≥1 ∪ { 0} denotes the spectrum of T

and for any a > 0ΠT (a)h := ∑

> i:λi≥a

⟨h, u i⟩Hui, h ∈ H ,

denotes the orthogonal projection onto the span of the eigenfunctions with eigenvalues at least a. We write Π[ h|V ] for the orthogonal projection of h onto a closed subspace V of H.Additionally, the Hausdorff distance between two sets A and B included in some metric space with metric 

d is defined as dH (A, B ) := max {sup x∈B d(x, A ), sup x∈A d(x, B )} where d(x, A ) := inf y∈A d(x, y ). 

Proof of Theorem 3. We prove the result under Assumption 7 part (a). The proof under part (b) is sym-metric, using the dual inverse problem. We begin with an informal sketch of the argument. The role of Assumption 7 is not to make the inverse problem well posed. Even under the uniform bound on the minimum-norm solution, the map 

P 7 → h†

> P

need not be continuous. Small changes in the law can still produce noticeable changes in the solution along directions that are only very weakly revealed by the conditional expectation operator. Thus, the proof cannot, and does not, proceed by showing that h† 

> Pj

converges to h† 

> P0

.The key observation is that continuity of the parameter requires less. The parameter does not depend on the whole function h† 

> P

in isolation. Rather, it depends on h† 

> P

only through its weighted average against the Riesz representer: φ(P ) = EP {αP (X, D )h† 

> P

(X, D )}. Thus, directions in which h† 

> P

changes can matter for the parameter only to the extent that they are also seen by αP . The proof exploits this distinction. The useful picture is the following. For a fixed value of D = d, decompose directions of h(X, d ) according to how strongly they are revealed by the conditional distribution of X given Z and D = d. Some directions are well revealed: changing h in those directions produces a visible change in the conditional mean E{h(X, d ) |

Z, D = d}. Other directions are weakly revealed: changing h in those directions produces only a very small change in this conditional mean. These weak directions are the source of ill-posedness, because solving the inverse problem requires amplifying what is only faintly seen in the observed law. The proof separates these two types of directions. First fix a threshold a > 0. Directions whose signal strength is at least a are treated as stable. On this stable part, the inverse problem behaves like an ordinary 146 finite-dimensional inverse problem with its singular values bounded away from zero. Combined with the uniform density bounds of Assumption 5 and the Hellinger continuity of the Riesz representer maps assumed in Theorem 3, along any sequence Pj → P0, the stable part of the minimum-norm solution varies continuously. This is the portion of the argument that shows that the truncated, well-identified component of h† 

> Pj

converges to the corresponding component of h† 

> P0

.The unstable part consists of the remaining directions, whose signal strength is below a. We do not try to show that the solution converges on this part. Indeed, in an ill-posed problem there is no reason to expect such convergence. The important point is instead that this unstable part is uniformly bounded in norm by Assumption 7. Therefore, although it may move around as j changes, it cannot become arbitrarily large. It remains to see why this bounded but potentially nonconvergent part does not affect the parameter. This is where the fixed function αP0 enters. Because αP0 belongs to the range of the adjoint inverse problem, it has no component in directions that are completely invisible. Moreover, since αP0 is square integrable, its total mass in sufficiently weak directions can be made arbitrarily small by choosing the threshold a small enough. Thus, even if the weak part of h† 

> Pj

does not converge, its inner product with αP0 is small: it is a bounded vector tested against a part of αP0 whose norm is small. The proof therefore follows a two-step logic. First choose the cutoff a small enough that the component of 

αP0 lying in directions weaker than a is negligible. Then, with this cutoff fixed, take j large enough that the stable parts of the inverse problems under Pj and P0 are close. The stable part is controlled by continuity of the well-conditioned finite-dimensional problem; the unstable part is controlled by the small tail of αP0

together with the uniform bound on h† 

> Pj

.Finally, the difference between integrating under Pj and integrating under P0 is handled separately. The uniform upper and lower density bounds in Assumption 5, together with Hellinger convergence, imply that the relevant marginal density ratios converge to one. The Hellinger continuity of the Riesz representer maps assumed in Theorem 3 gives convergence of the Riesz representers. Therefore, after rewriting the parameter under the fixed P0-inner product, the only nontrivial term is precisely the inner product between αP0 and 

h† 

> Pj

− h† 

> P0

, which is controlled by the stable/weak decomposition described above. The eight steps of the proof implement this strategy. Steps 1–3 put the problem on fixed P0-Hilbert spaces and show that, on these fixed spaces, the conditional expectation operators under Pj approach the operator under P0. Step 4 transfers the assumed uniform bound on the minimum-norm solutions to these fixed spaces. Step 5 proves that, after discarding the weakly identified directions below a fixed threshold, the stable parts of the solutions converge. Step 6 then uses the small spectral tail of αP0 , together with the uniform bound from Step 4, to show that the remaining weak directions do not contribute to the relevant inner product. Step 7 handles the change from integration under Pj to integration under P0, using the 147 convergence of the density ratios and of the Riesz representers. Finally, Step 8 combines these ingredients to show that φ(Pj ) − φ(P0) → 0. Thus the proof follows exactly the heuristic logic above: control the stable part by continuity, control the unstable part by boundedness and a small tail of αP0 , and then translate this inner-product control into continuity of the parameter. We are now ready to start the proof. Let {Pj }j≥1 ⊆ P cont be any sequence such that H(Pj , P 0) −→ 0. We must show that φ(Pj ) −→ φ(P0).

Throughout the proof, let pj denote the density of Pj with respect to the common dominating product measure λ from Assumption 5. By Assumption 5, there exists a constant Lbounded > 1, independent of j,such that

Proof of Theorem 7. We first give an informal proof sketch. Under the conditions of the theorem, in par-ticular either injectivity of EP0 together with a uniform bound on the norm of the minimum norm solution to the primal inverse problem, or a uniform bound on the norm of its β-source representer, h† 

> Pt

> P0

> Pt

> Pt

> Pt

Proof of Theorem 6. The proofs under the two conditions of Theorem 6 are identical once we have demon-strated that in either case, h† 

> Pt

converges weakly to h† 

> P0

.We first provide an informal proof sketch. Recall that we must establish that for any QMD at P0

159 submodel the derivative ddt φ(Pt) exists, and that the local parameter is bounded and linear in the score of the submodels. We begin by showing that weak convergence of h† 

> Pt

to h† 

> P0

is implied by the conditions of the Theorem. We then express {φ(Pt) − φ(P0)}/t as the sum of several inner products, each of which pairs a strongly convergent sequence with either a fixed function or the weakly convergent h† 

> Pt

. We then show that this limit is bounded and linear in the score of the submodel, which establishes pathwise differentiability. Finally, we use the hypotheses that ˜m(W, X, D ; h† 

> P0

) and m(W, Z, D ; qP0 ) are square integrable to rewrite this limit as an inner product against the hypothesized gradient, which identifies it as a gradient. These last two hypotheses are used only in this final step: they bear on the form of the gradient, not on whether φ is pathwise differentiable.

S.4.7 Proofs for Section 4.2 

Proof of Theorem 4. Recall that for a parameter to be pathwise differentiable relative to the set of all one-dimensional QMD at P0 submodels of Ph† 

> Pbound

, the derivative 

ddt φ(Pt)|t=0 

must exist for every such submodel, and there must exist a bounded linear operator ˙ φ : T (P0, Ph† 

> Pbound

) →

R, called the local parameter, satisfying ˙φ[s] = ddt φ(Pt)

∣∣∣∣t=0 

for s the score of {Pt : t ∈ [0 , δ )}.We show failure of pathwise differentiability by exhibiting a family of submodels {{ Pt,γ : t ∈ [0 , δ (γ)) }, γ ∈

(a1, a 2)} for which either the derivative ddt φ(Pt)∣∣t=0 does not exist for some γ, or for which there does not exist an operator satisfying ˙φ[sγ ] = ddt φ(Pt,γ )∣∣t=0 .Our construction relies on exhibiting a family of QMD at P0 submodels Pcounter whose scores sγ do not depend on γ, but for which the derivatives ddt φ(Pt)∣∣t=0 , if they exist, do depend on γ. The construction is similar in spirit to a similar construction in the proof of Theorem 5. We perturb the singular system of P0

to construct a new distribution Pt,γ , for which h† 

> Pt,γ

does not limit to h† 

> P0

, and for which the norm of the dual solution qPt,γ diverges. We then demonstrate that this Pt,γ ∈ P counter belongs to Ph†  

> Pbound

for each γ

and for t small enough. We show as claimed that the score of the submodel {Pt,γ ; t ∈ [0 , δ (γ)) } ⊆ P counter 

is not a function of γ, but that the derivative ddt φ(Pt)∣∣t=0 is, if it exists, thus proving the claimed failure of differentiability. As in related proofs, the majority of the argument consists of showing that Pt,γ is a distribution and belongs to Ph† 

> Pbound

.

Preliminaries: In the following arguments, we let λW , λ X , λ Z , λ D be normalized so that 

λW (Supp [PW ;0 ]) = λX (Supp [PX;0 ]) = λZ (Supp [PZ;0 ]) = λD (Supp [PD;0 ]) = 1 to avoid tracking un-necessary constants, as in Remark S.2. 165 We now define some useful objects and record some of their properties for later use. Let de ∈ Supp [PD;0 ]be such that αP0 (·, d e), r P0 (·, d e) are not P0-a.e. constant functions, which exists by assumption. Let 

u⊥;de ∈ N (EP0;de ) ∩ L∞(PX|de;0 ) with ∥u⊥;de ∥L2(PX|de ;0 ) = 1 .

We let ( σi;de , u i;de , v i;de )∞ 

> i=1

denote a singular system for EP0;de , with singular values arranged in non-increasing order. We take this singular system to include only singular values greater than 0, and to satisfy (27), which is possible by assumption. Because PX|de;0 is a probability measure, ∥ui;de ∥L∞(PX|de ;0 ) ≥∥ui;de ∥L2(PX|de ;0 ) = 1 for every i, so that the first sum in (27) additionally gives σi;de ∥vi;de ∥L∞(PZ|de ;0 ) → 0as i → ∞ .We now list some properties of this singular system. First, there are infinitely many such non-zero singular values. To see this, recall that E∗ 

> P0;de

is injective. As such, vi;de must form an orthonormal basis for L2(PZ|de ). On the other hand, L2(PZ|de ) is infinite dimensional because Z is continuously valued. As such, there are infinitely many non-zero singular values. On the other hand, the operator EP0 is compact by Assumption 5 (see the discussion in Carrasco, Florens, and Renault, 2007 after Assumption A.1), and so lim i→∞ σi;de = 0. Second, σ1; de , u1; de , and v1; de may all be taken equal to 1. To see this, recall that EP0;de is a contraction, so that σi;de ≤ 1. On the other hand, E∗

> P0;de

∥E P0;de u∥L2(PZ|de ;0 ) = ∥u∥L2(PX|de ;0 ) forces u(X) = g(Z) P0-a.s. given D = de for some g, which forces u to be a.e. constant because pX,Z |de;0 is bounded below on a rectangle by Assumption 5. The above choice is therefore unique up to a simultaneous sign flip of u1; de and v1; de .Finally, we let iα, i r > 1 be the first indices greater than 1 for which

Defining the distribution Pt,γ .

Having defined a perturbed conditional density for ( X, Z )|D = de, we now finish our construction of 

Pt,γ by defining the full joint density pt,γ . For d̸ = de, we let pX,Z |d;t,γ = pX,Z |d;0 and for all d we let 

Pt,γ (D = d) = P0(D = d). Then, as in the proof of Lemma S.1, we let 

pt,γ (w, x, z, d ) := p0(w, x, z, d ) + pW ;0 (w){pX,Z,D ;t,γ (x, z, d ) − pX,Z,D ;0 (x, z, d )}.

Following the proof of that lemma and uniform convergence of the conditional densities (S.98), we may choose 

δ(γ) sufficiently small, so that the above is a valid joint density for O, mutually absolutely continuous with P0,with the same rectangular support, and with density of ( X, Z, D ) under Pt,γ equal to pX,Z,D ;t,γ , satisfying Assumption 1, Assumption 4, and Assumption 5. Furthermore, pW,X,D ;t,γ = pW,X,D ;0 and pW,Z,D ;t,γ =

pW,Z,D ;0 , so that αPt,γ = αP0 and rPt,γ = rP0 . Furthermore, in light of the uniform convergence of pX,Z |de;t,γ 

to pX,Z |de;0 in (S.98), and because p0 is essentially bounded above by Lbounded and below by L−1

The score of {Pt,γ : t ∈ [0 , δ (γ)) }.

Proof of Theorem 5. Our proof proceeds by constructing a family of QMD at P0 submodels Pcounter := 

{{ Pt,γ : t ∈ [0 , δ )} ⊆ P h†  

> P,q †
> Pbound

, γ ∈ (a1, a 2)} for which ddt φ(Pt,γ )|t=0 exists for every γ, but is not a linear functional of the score of {Pt,γ : t ∈ [0 , δ )}; exhibiting such a family suffices to show that φ is not pathwise differentiable. Concretely, as in related proofs, we mix a singular pair of EP0 along which αP0 , r P0 are supported with a pair of null vectors of EP0 , E∗ 

> P0

, rotating each pair into the other by angle t so that both converge to their originals as t → 0. We then let the singular value paired with the (perturbed) null vectors vanish as t → 0at the same order, indexed by γ, as the vectors themselves rotate back toward the original pair; matching these orders is what keeps h† 

> Pt,γ

, q † 

> Pt,γ

bounded despite dividing by a vanishing singular value. The majority of the proof is dedicated to verifying that the resulting submodels {Pt,γ : t ∈ [0 , δ )} are valid probability distributions belonging to Ph†  

> P,q †
> Pbound

; the derivative and score computations that complete the argument appear at the end. In the following, we let λW , λ X , λ Z , λ D be normalized so that λW (Supp [PW ;0 ]) = λX (Supp [PX;0 ]) = 

λZ (Supp [PZ;0 ]) = λD (Supp [PD;0 ]) = 1 to avoid tracking unnecessary constants, as in Remark S.2. We now define our family of submodels. We begin with some preliminary definitions.
